This was a filial act on Uncle Charlie's part (as well as a brotherly and generous act); for though he fully agreed in the injustice of the Will, he was most anxious that his Father's name should not be dragged into Court. And he knew he could depend on your Father, who held both the above views with himself, to carry out his wishes in conjunction with the lawyer. The time was short, and the matter urgent; for my Brother had said he could not die happily until all was settled.*

*Your Father went to town next day - the lawyer and Counsel sat up all night to get the papers ready; and the following day Mr. Rhodes took the Will to Buxton to read to your Uncle and get his signature. This was done in the presence of your Aunt [Fannie](Hannah%20Frances%20Lister%20%28b.1843%29.md), Father, Mr. Eddy - Vicar of Buxton - and Mr. Rhodes of course. Dear Uncle seemed greatly relieved when this business was over; and your Father helped him dress in his Indian 'chaga' etc. and wheeled him into the adjoining Drawing Room on a sofa. But a few hours later, a collapse came on, and he began to sink, and peacefully* **{p81}<a name="81"></a>** *passed away about midnight. He was a man of few words, but told your Father he knew all was well. His was a case such as is described by [Micah in Chap.VI.8](http://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Micah#Chapter_6). We laid him to rest at Buxton, and the verse on his white marble cross is 'We asked life of Thee, and Thou gavest him a long life - even length of days for ever and ever.' [Ps.21.4](http://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Psalms#Psalm_21).*

*After the funeral we left Buxton, and spent the remainder of July* [1876] *at [Matlock](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matlock,_Derbyshire); Aunt [Fannie](Hannah%20Frances%20Lister%20%28b.1843%29.md) was with us, and two of her children. August at Retford, in Nottinghamshire; and September at [Southwold](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?q=southwold&ie=UTF8&hq=&hnear=Southwold,+Suffolk,+United+Kingdom&gl=uk&ei=7UPyTLqbEsHChAe91-XxCw&oi=geocode_result&ved=0CCIQ8gEwAA&z=13), where [Uncle Claude Thornton](Rev.%20Claude%20Cecil%20Thornton%20%281844-1921%29.md), and your [Aunt Fanny](Fanny%20Barton%20%281841-1878%29.md) his wife, then lived at the Vicarage. She was a very fine woman, both in looks and in character; and her five children were of the same ages as our, from Jack to Jessie. I think this was the last time we saw her - for her children alas! were left motherless less than 18 months later.*

*In October* [1876] *we took a furnished house in* *[Strathmore Gardens](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?q=Strathmore+Gardens,+Kensington&ie=UTF8&hq=&hnear=Strathmore+Gardens,+Greater+London,+United+Kingdom&gl=uk&ei=qT_9TNDBB4bLhAf6-PiKDg&oi=geocode_result&ved=0CBoQ8gEwAA&z=16)* *a few doors off my dearest [Mother](Emily%20Dougan%20%281806-1877%29.md), who had made a home there for herself and Aunt [Mary](Mary%20Elliott%20%28b.1844%29.md) and fitted it up with [Tattingstone](Tattingstone%20Rectory%2C%20Suffolk.md) furniture. There was a good deal of vigour about the dear Mother's mind and manner, though she was much crippled with rheumatisms and gout in her eyelids made her almost blind. Many of her watercolour and pencil [sketches](_Emily%20Dougan%27s%201856%20Sketchbook.md) are known to you all but her clever hands could no longer paint or work. Yet she never allowed herself to be dull - because she had a wonderful gift for throwing herself into the interests of others and of living in their lives; and of picturing their friends and surroundings which she had never seen, nor would see. This true unselfishness made her greatly beloved by all who knew her. Outwardly she might have been thought a wreck of a lovely, and in some ways a brilliant woman - she certainly was this last in conversation, and in linguistic talent; and in the rapidity with which she read and mastered books so long as her eyesight lasted; as well as in her remarkable memory, for we often tried in vain to puzzle her with a question in history. But spiritually and intellectually there were no signs of age about her. She liked to keep up with all the best books of the day - and was read aloud to much by Aunt Mary and by all of us in turn. She greatly enjoyed music, and when the Canes were staying with her, and we had the help of Uncle [Jack](Rev.%20John%20Brattle%20Cane%20%281829-1896%29.md)'s sweet tenor voice, we managed a good deal together. Her powers of learning* **{p82}<a name="82"></a>** *by heart too were first rate - so that within a week of her death she and Aunt Mary used to see who could learn a chapter in the Epistles first! Mother drove out now in a brougham most days, and I was often her companion, not being able for much walking.*

*We had a bright Christmas with her, and our children delighted her with carols. And then - a slight chill taken brought on bronchitis, and after only three days in bed, God took her Home, on January 3rd 1877. It was a crushing and sudden blow to us all; for there seemed no reason why she should not have enjoyed some years of love, joy and peace surrounded by her children, and their children. But it was all gain for her to go hence - and she smiled when we reminded her shortly before the end that her [Arthur](Arthur%20Wilmot%20Elliott%20%281841-1862%29.md) had said 'I will be at the Gate to welcome you, Mother'. We laid her in the vault at [Tattingstone](Tattingstone%20Rectory%2C%20Suffolk.md)* [Church]*, where our [Father](Rev.%20Charles%20Boileau%20Elliott%20%281803-1875%29.md), and [Grandparents](Charles%20Elliott%20%281776-1856%29.md), and Uncle [William Elliott](William%20Henry%20Elliott%20%281811-1870%29.md) all lie, and with her 'passing' the Chapter of my early life seems to close.*

*The two houses in Strathmore Gardens were now broken up; and your Father accepted a charge at Harrow, and took duty at [Rosceth Church](http://www.ccrharrow.org/); and we had a lovely house there for nine months called 'The Mount' - since turned into a Nunnery! There on April 5th* [1877] *our sixth son was born; and we called you [Claude William Boileau](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md), my 'Benjamin'. Mr. [Henry Wright](http://www.thepeerage.com/p35719.htm#i357186), Uncle [Claude](Rev.%20Claude%20Cecil%20Thornton%20%281844-1921%29.md) and Aunt [Fanny](Fanny%20Barton%20%281841-1878%29.md) were your sponsors.*

*It was the beginning of this year* [1877] *that Pryke came to us, as cook. She was then a young widow if 27 with one delicate boy. As you all know she has been in the family almost ever since; partly with us, and partly at [Thornhaugh](Thornhaugh%2C%20Croydon.md) - and is (as I write, **1903**) now living with [Will](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) at [Rawdon](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rawdon,_West_Yorkshire), as Housekeeper.*

*That summer* [1877] *we had a Missionary Garden Party at the Mount, and gathered many of the gentry around us, to hear [Bishop Crowther](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Ajayi_Crowther), of Sierra Leone speak. The 'little ones' came down later in the afternoon; and I well remember my confusion when [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) - aged three then - gave a terrified scream on catching sight of his coal black face! which I had to cover by flying with her into the garden. Next day too, [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) was found in tears; because on the Bishop's departure, when Arthur returned to the bedroom from which he had been ousted for a night, he thought he would have to sleep in the same sheets as the Bishop, and that the black would come off!!* **{p83}<a name="83"></a>**

# Settling in Cambridge, 1877-1881

*This was the first summer we took you all to [Cromer](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cromer); and thence Father and I went one day to look at [Trinity Vicarage, Cambridge](Holy%20Trinity%20Church%2C%20Cambridge.md#Brookside), to decide whether he should take charge of Mr. [Charles Simeon](Famous%20connections.md#Charles%20Simeon)'s old Church - [Holy Trinity](Holy%20Trinity%20Church%2C%20Cambridge.md). This was eventually agreed upon; and in October of this year* [1877] *we furnished, and settled down in, our first English home; and I know it was a very happy and interesting home to all of you dear ones, as well as to ourselves for 16½ years; for we did not leave it until February 1894. It was a large roomy house; 14 bedrooms, 6 sitting rooms; with a Drawing Room planned by our dear old patron, Mr. Peache, to suit large gatherings of Undergraduates; and when our friend [Sir Arthur Blackwood](http://www.thepeerage.com/p3308.htm#i33076) gave an address in that room in November to 80 men as our 'house warming', it was the first of very many that took place there during those 16 years.*

*Looking back one realizes with sorrow how much was left undone - and how much more might have been done. Yet I feel bound to record here our deep thankfulness that we were allowed to fill our lives all these many years full for others; and that we were given the health, the means, and the desire to use our great opportunities amongst hundreds of young men at the University, during this critical time in their lives. We came to that work with eight children; the eldest 13, the youngest 6 months. Aunt [Mary](Mary%20Elliott%20%28b.1844%29.md) was now a member of our family too; and when the following year [Isa Young](Isabel%20Mary%20Young%20%28b.1870%29.md) from India joined our schoolroom party, our household numbered 20 - including our two selves and a governess.*

*Besides this large establishment, we had a parish of 2000 a mile or more off; which had many calls upon us. And finally we had a large, and steadily increasing family of 'Sons in the University', as I have said; never less than 200 on our books, introduced by parents, guardians or clergy; and all were invited to the [Vicarage](Holy%20Trinity%20Church%2C%20Cambridge.md#Brookside) two or more times every term. Father's advent in Cambridge was the beginning of a new era there as regards interest in Foreign Missions. He speedily started the plan of having a collector in every College, amongst the men themselves, for Missions; and at the close of each term, these men brought their boxes or lists to Father to shew how they were getting on. The result was not only a great increase in funds derived from the University; but the increase in offers of personal service was still more marked; the number of offers to C.M.S. alone during the next few years amounting to 140.* **{p84}<a name="84"></a>**

*Our friend Arthur Shields who gave 15 years to the Mission Field, has told us that it was a remark of Father's on one of these occasions which sent him out. Putting his hand on Shields' shoulder, after he had brought up his collection from [Jesus College](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_College,_Cambridge) to Father's study one day, the Father said 'Why don't you go yourself, Shields?' For years Father Had a Bible Reading for young men in his study once a week - and two or three times a year large gatherings in our Drawing Room to hear such men as Bishops Hannington, Poole, and Stirling; Col. H. McGregor R.E. - Mr. Sholto Douglas - Sir A. Blackwood - Mr. Evan Hopkins - Webb-Peploe - C. A. Fox - Hay Aiken and others.*

*One afternoon a week during Term, we were always At Home to Undergraduates; and with the help of little books of notes which I called my 'cribs' I easily learnt to remember faces, names and initials, parentage and homes, Colleges and lodgings, tripos and sports of each. Sunday evenings too we kept open house for men - and 25 to 30 often came up to coffee and singing. We wondered more houses were not thus opened to Undergraduates when we heard that tutors and proctors said more men got into mischief on Sunday night than on any other day, from not knowing what to do with themselves. As years went on, and you dear boys were absent at school nine months out of twelve, and our table less full, we used to ask men in to breakfast, lunch and dinner, two, three or four at a time, and seldom had a meal alone, or a half hour that we could call our own. Thank God for the youth and health and strength to live such a life - we could not do it now - and I often wonder how we did it then.*

*Our dear old Church — [Holy Trinity](Holy%20Trinity%20Church%2C%20Cambridge.md)* *—* *dating from the 12th Century - underwent much restoration during the next few years; and your Father's tact and smile got over any difficulty experienced in small changes he felt it right to make in the Services. Doors with locks to them were first removed from the seats; and then high pews cut down. The organ loft and one gallery were taken away, and organ put into transept; the chancel re-pewed in oak. The Psalms chanted, and Amens sung; surplice in pulpit etc. Mr. [Death](http://www.cambridge.gov.uk/ccm/content/council-and-democracy/how-the-council-works/the-mayor/cambridge-mayors-1870-1880.en), then Mayor, gave £500 for an East window in 1887 in memory of Queen Victoria's Jubilee; and Mrs. Death gave an oak eagle lectern later, as a thank offering for Father's safe return after a four months trip to [Ceylon](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lanka) (in 1884) to smooth over matters between C.M.S. and Bishop Copplestone.* **{p85}<a name="85"></a>** *(This last event, by the way, was engraven on [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md)'s memory in an amusing way, as I have since discovered; for she, being then ten, was set to write out a copy 12 times for some peccadillo committed; and the words given her to write were "Father is a peace-maker"!) Later on a new pulpit was given by the Deaths; and [Charles Simeon](Famous%20connections.md#Charles%20Simeon)'s old mahogany one converted into a cabinet to contain books in the New Vestry. Ladies in our congregation worked long and short kneelers for the East End; and I worked an altar cloth which was a great pleasure.*

*The autumn of 1877 had been [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md)'s first term at [Haileybury](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haileybury_and_Imperial_Service_College), and [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) joined him there the spring of 1879. Arthur, after 3 years at Haileybury, began to coach for the Army - and went to the [Oxford Military College](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford_Military_College) for a year; thence to a crammer, Mr. Wolfram at Blackheath; and got his Commission in 1885. Jack, after six happy years at Haileybury, entered that same year at [Pembroke College, Cambridge](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pembroke_College,_Cambridge). Whilst [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md), after 3 years of day school in Cambridge, went to [Monckton Coombe](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monkton_Combe_School), near Bath, in 1880; and spent from 1883 to 1886 at [Highgate School](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highgate_School), coming up to [St. John's College](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_John's_College,_Cambridge) in 1886.*

*[Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md), who was seven when our Cambridge life began, was the eldest of our Schoolroom party, then presided over by Emmie Smyth as governess. [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) was Cecil's companion there; and a year later they were joined by [Isa Young](Isabel%20Mary%20Young%20%28b.1870%29.md). [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md), [Douglas](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) and [Billy](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) formed the Nursery trio. In 1880 we were advised to send Cecil to live by the sea on account of his poor foot; and he spent three years with Mrs. Vines at [Ramsgate](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramsgate), and went daily to the South Eastern College for his lessons. He was then 10; and when after six months, he brought home a prize for Scripture knowledge, he told us he felt sure he owed it to having been so well grounded at home in Mrs. Mortimer's later books 'The Captivity of Babylon', and 'Kings of Judah and Israel'. In 1884 Cecil joined [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) at [Highgate](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highgate_School); and in 1887 or 1888 he too came up to the University, and entered [Trinity Hall](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Hall,_Cambridge).*

*You three elder Brothers were always good at every kind of sport; but [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md), to whom this was denied, had music as his compensation. From a child he had shewn real talent in this way; and as he grew up, both Organ and Piano were a great resource to him, and have been so increasingly.*

*And now I must look back for a spell.* **{p86}<a name="86"></a>**

*In 1879 or '80 we saw the foundation stone laid of [Ridley Hall](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridley_Hall) - at Newnham – and your Father being one of the Council of this new Theological College, persuaded Mr. [Handley Moule](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md), after some difficulty, to accept the post of Principal of this Hall, when opened, and to combine with it the Evening Lectureship at Holy Trinity Church. He was well known in Cambridge, having been Dean of Trinity College for some years - but had given up this post three years previously in order to act as curate to his revered old Father at Dorchester - whose death in 1860 set his son free to return to Cambridge; and many both amongst 'town and gown' rejoiced at his return.*

*In January 1881 'Ridley' was opened with its first 8 graduate students. [Canon Girdlestone](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Baker_Girdlestone), who was one of the large company present on the occasion, when congratulating Father on his success in persuading the new Principal to accept the post, added (prophetically!) "the next thing you must do is to get him a good wife"! The remark was not made seriously - and at the time nothing seemed more unlikely; and yet, as you all know, that very year, in August 1881, he married your Aunt [Mary](Mary%20Elliott%20%28b.1844%29.md) and became your 'Uncle [Handley](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md)' - and has ever since enriched our family by his prayers, his sermons, and many other gifts; and has proved a 'Brother indeed' - for no man living loves your Father so much.*

*After their marriage [Isa](Isabel%20Mary%20Young%20%28b.1870%29.md) went to live at* [Ridley Hall](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridley_Hall)*, and she and [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) and [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) went daily to the [Girls' Perse School](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perse_School_for_Girls) for some years; thence Isa and Ethel went to [Vevey](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vevey) to school together; and later on, the first went to [Cheltenham College](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheltenham_Ladies'_College), and the last to [Brownshill Court](http://www.britishlistedbuildings.co.uk/en-133552-brownshill-court-painswick), near Stroud, until 1888 and 1889 when School days ceased for both.*

*I should not forget to mention that in April 1881 Uncle [Willy Young](Sir%20William%20Mackworth%20Young%20%28b.1840%29.md) married 'Aunt [May](Frances%20May%20Egerton.md)' - and in 1884 they came home together - and again in 1888; after which last visit they took [Isa](Isabel%20Mary%20Young%20%28b.1870%29.md) back with them to India.*

# British holidays, 1878-1882

*Our summer holiday outings were such a marked feature of every year, that I must chronicle these; and perhaps it may have a rejuvenating effect on some of you to recall those weeks in years to come. The dear Father was anxious so soon as you Boys were old enough, to pass on to you, and share with you, his own early love for the English [Lake District](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_District); its Hills and Dales and Lakes; and he knew it well, for he had botanized much in this* **{p87}<a name="87"></a>** *country-side during the fifties. He also taught you early to study, and make maps of the country you walked, and later cycled through - thus encouraging a topographical knowledge of your own land first and foremost. We had both of us realised the educational advantages of travel in other countries as well as our own; and as far as able, carried this out. Our rule was not to go two summers running to the same place for our holidays; but we gave a great preference to the English Lakes having visited them ten times between 1878 and 1901.*

*1870 was my own first sight of [Windermere](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windermere) and [Ambleside](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambleside), where Father and I spent a week or two near [Waterhead](http://www.visitcumbria.com/amb/waterhead-pier.htm). But I believe 1878 was the first year we took you three elder Boys there - and had Willy and Arthur Storrs with us at the same time; about your ages. Just before you young people joined us, we had been present at one of the earliest of the [Conventions](http://www.biblebelievers.com/moody/15.html) started by Canon Battersby at [Keswick](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keswick,_Cumbria). These gatherings began in a field at the foot of the Rectory garden, in a tent; and have been held annually now for nearly 30 years. They were the outcome of two previous Conventions held in 1874 and 1875 (while we were in India) at Oxford and Brighton, for the deepening of the spiritual life of believers; and to refresh and help those whose lives are mainly spent in teaching and in helping others. In those days 300 or 400 looked forward each year to spending ten days in the lovely Vale of Keswick (then a quiet retreat) for rest and teaching as to how to lead 'a higher holier life'.*

*Now the hundreds have become thousands; and although many feel that these occasions have been somewhat spoiled by numbers, and regret that the assemblies have not been more regulated by clergy of the Church of England, yet there can be no question that they have been fraught with a vast amount of blessing to both men and women; and amongst these to many clergy. The chief leaders of this movement after Canon Battersby have been Mr. Evan Hopkins, Mr. C. A. Fox, Mr. Webb-Peploe, Mr. Elder Cumming; and in later years your Uncle [Handley Moule](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) (who has been on the Keswick platform once since he was made Bishop of Durham) - and George Macgregor. Afternoon sylvan gatherings on [Friar's Crag](http://www.visitcumbria.com/kes/friars-crag.htm), or on one of the little islands on [Derwent Water](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derwentwater); and singing of well known hymns in boats on the Lake on beautiful summer evenings enhance the charm of the retrospect. '[Like a river glorious](http://library.timelesstruths.org/music/Like_a_River_Glorious/)' is perhaps the best known of the hymns whose harmonies we often heard floated across the waters, and joined in.* **{p88}<a name="88"></a>**

*August 1879 we were again at [Cromer](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cromer) - where [Colne House](http://www.britishlistedbuildings.co.uk/en-222510-colne-house-hotel-cromer/bingmap), the Dowager Lady Buxton's home, has for many years been a rendezvous for the great Buxton, Gurney and Hoare clans, and for many other privileged friends. The [myrtle](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myrtus) bushes, and [fuchias](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuschia) growing out in that garden, the latter 20 feet high, are remarkable; and as Cromer faces directly to the North Pole this greatly surprised me. Father says it is due to the soil. The proximity of country lanes, and of the heather-covered Lighthouse hill, and a Roman encampment on the moor near the sea shore, add greatly to the charms of Cromer; and at this stage in your lives you younger ones loved it much.*

*In 1880 we made a great departure, and went for June and July, with the 4 younger ones and [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md), to [Wemyss Bay](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wemyss_Bay) on the West coast of Scotland; and Father took duty at a little church there built by Mr. John Burns, afterwards Lord Inverclyde, who lived at Wemyss Castle; and which is now owned by his son. Aunt [Mary](Mary%20Elliott%20%28b.1844%29.md), and [Isa](Isabel%20Mary%20Young%20%28b.1870%29.md) (who has always called herself 'No.9' in the family) were also of our party; also Pryke and Pratt and Nurse and parlourmaid and Emmie Smyth. I have a very happy remembrance of that two months. You two sisters were dressed in scarlet petticoats and dark blue sailor blouses; and [Douglas](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) and [Willie](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) in scarlet knickers and blue jerseys. I fancy I can see you all tripping barefoot on the rocks after sea weed; or Isa and [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) either side of Pratt bathing from the beach.*

*The outline of [Arran](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Arran) is very beautiful from Wemyss Bay, and we spent one lovely day on the Island; and found [fuchia](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuschia) hedges growing like privet or laurel, and rare ferns and flowers for which [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) had a sharp eye which pleased your botanical Father. I may mention here, that before he was 20, Father (together with [Aunt Rick](Josephina%20Rickman%20%281808-1892%29.md)) made a very interesting collection of wild flowers and dried and classified and named 1300 out of 1500 of those that grow in the British Isles; the mahogany cabinet containing which he has now given to [Willie](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md). He also made a large collection of Indian ferns when in that country.*

*Aunt [Alicia](Alicia%20Elliott%20%281832-1912%29.md) and Uncle [Jack Cane](Rev.%20John%20Brattle%20Cane%20%281829-1896%29.md) paid us a visit at Wemyss bay, and we went for a two days trip to [Inverary](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inverary) and [Oban](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oban); and thence to the two interesting Islands of [Staffa](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staffa) and [Iona](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iona). The first with its basaltic caves into which we rowed in a small boat, amidst columns like those of a Cathedral aisle. The latter island hallowed by the memory of [St. Columba](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Columba), the first English Bishop who brought the Gospel to our Northern Isle* **{p89}<a name="89"></a>** *from the East, in the year 563 and who lies buried there, with many other early Christians. A very beautiful Iona cross, date about 300 A.D. is the original of one we often see copied - and is in wonderfully good preservation, with a crucifix cut in it.*

*At the end of this July 1880 Father and I went again to [Keswick](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keswick,_Cumbria) - the younger ones returning to Cambridge; and [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) and [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) met us there from [Haileybury](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haileybury_and_Imperial_Service_College). Father enjoyed great walks with them, and pioneered them over most of the Hill tops in turn: [Great Gable](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Gable), [Grisdale Pike](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grisedale_Pike), [Skiddaw](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skiddaw) etc.*

*In June 1881 Father went to Switzerland, and was at [Bex](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bex) with dear Uncle [Jack Cane](Rev.%20John%20Brattle%20Cane%20%281829-1896%29.md); and in August that year as I have before said, Aunt [Mary](Mary%20Elliott%20%28b.1844%29.md) was married to Uncle [Handley](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md); after which great family event we spent a quiet time at a village called [Bourne](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourn) nine miles from Cambridge which was not very interesting.*

*For July and August 1882 we all went to [Tattingstone Rectory](Tattingstone%20Rectory%2C%20Suffolk.md); and Father took duty for my cousin Mr. R. Hawkins, in my dear old Home. The [Moules](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) paid us a little visit there; and it was a great pleasure to see many of my dear ones around us in a spot which must ever be peopled by the memory of dear ones 'gone before'; and of my own childhood and girlhood, for I was born there. It is the garden that I love best - with its deodara avenue; and rustic flower-covered arches; its fine ibex and copper beech on the big lawn; and long shrubbery walk leading up to the Church where sleep our blessed dead until the Resurrection Morn. The Park, too, across the road is delightful with its fine witch-elms, and swans and wild fowl on the waters.*

# Moody & Sankey's visit to Cambridge, 1882

*During November this year (1882) Mr. [Moody](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_L._Moody), the American Evangelist, held a Mission in Cambridge which was a unique experience. Although he had been actually invited to the University by the Undergraduates, a large number of young men were prepared to mob Messrs. Moody and [Sankey](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ira_D._Sankey) on the first evening. There were said to be 1700 young men in the Corn Exchange, 300 or more of whom were singing rowdy songs before the speakers came on to the platform. As soon as these gentlemen appeared, they were greeted with derisive shouts, and jokes from these ill-mannered youths; until half a dozen Proctors and M.A.s present (your Father being one of them) walked down the Hall in cap and gown, and picking out the ring leaders, quietly ordered them out, and thus restored silence. This apparently bad* **{p90}<a name="90"></a>** *beginning, wraught for good in the end; for the more gentlemanly men (though not yet at all of Mr. Moody's way of thinking) were so indignant at their companions' rude behaviour, that they got up a memorial to apologize to Mr. Moody; and Mr. Lauder (recently consecrated Bishop of Hong Kong) who presented the apology became a changed man from that day. In addition to special addresses to men, there was one general meeting each night for All; and as I sang in the choir, it was a wonderful sight to watch the rapt attention of hundreds of upturned faces, as that remarkable 'Apostle' from the far West arrested and held them by his simple racey style; his only eloquence being that of a Heaven-sent Power. Such men as Oscar Browning, Fellow of Kings, Welldon (now Bishop), Ryle (also Bishop), Appleton and others, sat daily under Mr. Moody; and other 'dons' also. The 'After-Meetings' held nightly in the Annexe for speaking individually to those who desired guidance and help, were crowded. Most of the 'Cambridge Seven' who went out the following year for the China Inland Mission, found these gatherings the beginning of a new life to them. Amongst these were the brother cricketers Stanley-Smith - Charles and J. K. Studd - Pohill - Turner - Beauchamp and very many others; including a remarkable Japanese named Wadagaki. Mr. [Sankey](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ira_D._Sankey) was the first to prove at this time, by his Solos and Quartetts, the power of the Ministry of Song at such services; and his methods, from their unconventionality and warmth and reality reached the hearts of all classes rich and poor; in all the greatest towns of England and Scotland - including London where - in one of our great Theatres - even some of the Royal Family went to hear the Evangelist.*

# Nine months in Europe, 1882-1883

*This Mission in Cambridge gave a great deal of work to the Clergy; and when it was over, Father quite broke down; and Sir George Paget* [brother of [James Paget](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Paget)?] *ordered him abroad for several months. Thus it came to pass that on December 22nd* [1882] *ten of the family migrated to [Brussels](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels) for a month, en route to Switzerland, and did not return to England for nine months. Brussels is a bright, clean, beautiful town; but Father was so poorly all the time, that I was not sorry to move on at the end of January 1883 to [Clarens](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarens,_Switzerland), on Lake of Geneva. [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) returned to school at [Haileybury](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haileybury_and_Imperial_Service_College); and [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) and [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) were at [Monckton Coombe](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monkton_Combe_School) and Ramsgate respectively. We placed [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) for three months in a family at [Montreux](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montreux) to learn French; and settled down at* **{p91}<a name="91"></a>** *'Maison Martin' a Villa above [Clarens](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarens,_Switzerland), for four months, with Carol Mathews, who had now been with us a year; and Ellen Jacklin as house parlourmaid; and a Swiss cook.*

*We sent the 'four little ones' to a French Day School; and when snow and ice abounded, it was great fun to toboggan down our long hill to School every morning; Father generally steering. As the winter passed and the snow melted, numberless wild flowers spring into life and blossom, and our little Chalet was surrounded by a many-coloured carpet. Gradually the dear Father regained strength and tone, and thoroughly enjoyed the life. Every afternoon he took you dear children long walks - with his botany case slung over his shoulder; and brought back treasures to make our rooms gay — first with the wild [crocus](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocus), then [hepatica](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hepatica); later [anemones](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anemone) white, mauve, and scarlet, and sulphur. [Snow-flakes](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leucojum), '[primula farinosa](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primula_farinosa)', golden [adonis](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adonis_(plant)), [globe flower](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globe-flower), [monkshood](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monkshood), [gagea](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gagea) followed; besides fields white with [narcissi](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narcissus_(plant)), which also covered the hillsides, and strongly flavoured the cows' milk! and several varieties of [gentian](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentian). Many of these Father dried, and has preserved; and others which would not retain their colour, I painted. I had half a dozen lessons from a girl at Clarens - the only ones I ever had; and painted over 40 wild flowers that spring. At Easter dear Uncle [Jack](Rev.%20John%20Brattle%20Cane%20%281829-1896%29.md) and Aunt [Alicia](Alicia%20Elliott%20%281832-1912%29.md) came out to pay us a visit for a fortnight, and also Uncle [Handley](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) and his brother Charles Moule. We took a carriage and made a two or three days excursion in the [Simmenthal](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simmental) with the two last; and revisited [Rosinière](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rossini%C3%A8re), where I had been 20 years before with our [Parents](Rev.%20Charles%20Boileau%20Elliott%20%281803-1875%29.md) and Aunt [Alicia](Alicia%20Elliott%20%281832-1912%29.md).*

*You dear children and Carol had put up a lovely big WELCOME on the Chalet, in [oxlips](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primula_elatior), in letters a foot and a half high, on our return. You were all great walkers; and though [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) and [Will](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) were then only 6½ and 5 years old they walked as well as any; and nearly reached the summit of the [Rocher du Naye](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rochers_de_Naye) once; and thoroughly imbibed their Father’s keen delight in flowers. To make sure of not overwalking the younger ones, Father let Guy lead and set the pace. Mr. Ormsby was Chaplain of Clarens at this time for many years; and the whole family were very kind and friendly.*

*In June* [1883] *we moved to [Ballaigues](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballaigues) on the [Jura Mountains](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jura_Mountains) for three months; whence we had wondrous views of the [Dent du Midi](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dents_du_Midi); and Morchs; and of the* **{p92}<a name="92"></a>** *[Oberland Alps](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernese_Alps), and even of [Mont Blanc](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mont_Blanc). The lovely sunset hues that these snow-capped mountains took on, made even little [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) exclaim one evening 'The roseate hues of early dawn, the brightness of the day; the crimson of the sunset sky, how fast they fade away'!*

*In August* [1883] *the three schoolboys came out from England for their holidays; and we sent [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md), [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) and [Will](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) home with Carol, and kept only four with us. We visited Orbe Chaumont and [Neufchatel](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuch%C3%A2tel); and I remember a Nail factory which greatly interested the Boys - also the Clock and Watch and Musical box factory at a lovely place called [St. Croix](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sainte-Croix,_Switzerland), in the Hills. On leaving Ballaigues, and en route to England, we took them to see [Lausanne](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lausanne), [Clarens](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarens,_Switzerland) and [Chillon](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chillon); and also [Berne](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berne), [Interlaken](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interlaken) and [Grindelwald](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grindelwald).*

# Assorted travels, 1883-1890

*By the middle of September* [1883] *we were once more in our Cambridge home, and found Aunt [Mary](Mary%20Elliott%20%28b.1844%29.md)'s little [Tessa](Tesie%20Moule%20%281882-1905%29.md), now 10 months old, at Ridley Hall, had grown apace during our absence.*

*The summer of 1884 we spent a month at [Rednock](http://www.welcometoscotland.com/accommodation/self-catering-apartments-holiday-cottage-and-caravans/loch-lomond-stirling/castle-rednock-farm-1), on the [Lake of Monteath](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_of_Menteith) N. B.* [North of the border?]*, the home of an early friend of Father's, Mr. Graham Sheppard. [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) was 20 this year, and we gave him a gun, and he shot his first game with the keeper; and sent you four 'little ones', who were at Cromer with Aunt [Bessie](Elizabeth%20Barton%20%28b.1832%29.md), his first brace of grouse; which she, over-generously, shared with the [Causton](Josephina%20Barton%20%28b.1840%29.md) family much to your chagrin! This August we heard that Arthur had passed into [Sandhurst](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Military_Academy_Sandhurst); where in September. he took up his abode, until the following August when he got his commission.*

![John Barton and his family at Holy Trinity Vicarage Cambridge 1884 Back row Ethel Jack Cecil Jessie Fred Centre row Arthur Emily John In front Bill Guy.jpg](../files/John%20Barton%20and%20his%20family%20at%20Holy%20Trinity%20Vicarage%20Cambridge%201884%20Back%20row%20Ethel%20Jack%20Cecil%20Jessie%20Fred%20Centre%20row%20Arthur%20Emily%20John%20In%20front%20Bill%20Guy.jpg "John Barton and his family at Holy Trinity Vicarage Cambridge 1884 Back row Ethel Jack Cecil Jessie Fred Centre row Arthur Emily John In front Bill Guy.jpg")\
Captioned '[Trinity Vicarage](Holy%20Trinity%20Church%2C%20Cambridge.md#Brookside) 1884'. Back row: [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) (aged ~13), [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) (~20), [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) (~14), [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) (~10?!) and [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) (~16). Front row: [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) (~20), [Bill](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) (~7), [Emily](Emily%20Elliott%20%281839-1924%29.md) (~45), [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) (~9) and [John](Rev.%20John%20Barton%20of%20Cambridge%20%281836-1908%29.md) (~48?!). Possibly it was taken later than 1884, and mislabelled?

*This autumn of '84 we had three cases of [scarlet fever](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scarlet_fever) at [Trinity Vicarage](Holy%20Trinity%20Church%2C%20Cambridge.md#Brookside); [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md), [Willie](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) and a housemaid all came out in the rash on the same evening. Carol, [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) and [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) went immediately into rooms in [Trumpington Street](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?q=trumpington+street+cambridge&ie=UTF8&hq=&hnear=Trumpington+St,+Cambridge+CB2,+United+Kingdom&gl=uk&ei=a9wATf2FAYqIhQfC0pHtBw&oi=geocode_result&ved=0CBoQ8gEwAA&z=16), and we isolated the three infectious cases on the Nursery landing with a qualified Nurse. You four elder boys were fortunately away; but Father, who was on the point of starting on a Peace Mission to [Ceylon](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lanka) for 4 months where ructions had arisen between Bishop Copplestone and the C.M.S. men, felt great difficulty about leaving me; and had such a bad throat himself at the time, that the doctor could not certify his as being safe from infection. He decided to go, however, unless a fourth case developed in a given time; and as all went well, we faced the trial of separation at such an anxious time, and he sailed early in November,* **{p93}<a name="93"></a>** *leaving me alone at Trinity Vicarage with the servants and three invalids. Thank God all went well; and after two months we emerged from our seclusion into the world again; and after the most strenuous precautions against retaining infection in the house, we left Cambridge for a month or so, and went to [Hastings](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hastings), the four younger ones Carol and I; whilst Pryke was left in charge at Home to see whitewashing, sizeing, and painting carried on throughout the rooms on the Nursery floor; sulphur burnt in every room, and clothes fumigated therewith. Mattresses were all thrown out of the back windows, and taken away in iron hand-carts to be disinfected by order of the Sanitary Council; and every Nursery picture on the walls, burnt, alas! It was a great deal of trouble (and expense also); but I felt that in a house where there was so much coming end going as in ours it ought to be clear that if another case of fever appeared, infection had come from outside, and not inside.*

*In March 1885 the dearest Father returned after four months absence, having accomplished the end for which he was asked to go to Ceylon, by the Church Missionary Society; and left matters on an amicable footing between Bishop Copplestone and the Missionaries.*

*In July 1885 we had a delightful family gathering at [Colwyn Bay](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colwyn_Bay), a Welsh resort which will always be associated with dear '[Cam](John%20Campbell%20Barton%20%281858-1889%29.md)' who died in his prime four years later of [blackwater fever](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackwater_fever) in Sierra Leone. The [Mackworth Youngs](Sir%20William%20Mackworth%20Young%20%28b.1840%29.md), and their baby boys [Gerard](Gerard%20Mackworth%20Young%20%28b.1884%29.md) and [Hubert](Sir%20Hubert%20Egerton%20Young%20%281885-1950%29.md), and [Isa](Isabel%20Mary%20Young%20%28b.1870%29.md), you three elder Boys and [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) (at Cam's special invitation), Aunt [Emily Barton](Emily%20Barton%20%281842-1927%29.md), [Effie](Ellinor%20Josephina%20Barton%20%28b.1857%29.md) and [Alice](Alice%20Matilda%20Barton%20%28b.1862%29.md), besides the whole [Causton](Josephina%20Barton%20%28b.1840%29.md) clan, made a party of 30 in different houses. Most of us met for morning prayers at 'Penrhyn House' and for evenings of singing, where Uncle [Willy](Sir%20William%20Mackworth%20Young%20%28b.1840%29.md) conducted the large choir of voices; and joined in the many long excursions and picnics arranged by your Father and Aunt [Phena](Josephina%20Barton%20%28b.1840%29.md), who were the two enterprising spirits. '[Cam](John%20Campbell%20Barton%20%281858-1889%29.md)' was the idol of all the young ones; because he loved them, and had great sympathy with those on the threshold of life. He was on leave from his Regiment in [Jamaica](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamaica), and this autumn was a turning point in his life in more senses than one. He was keen on temperance too; having realized that alcohol is injurious, as well as an unnecessary luxury for young people in health; and made his cousins sew a bit of blue ribbon on* **{p94}<a name="94"></a>** *to all his coats. He stayed with us at [Trinity Vicarage](Holy%20Trinity%20Church%2C%20Cambridge.md#Brookside) later in the year, and drew [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) and [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) together as friends; and proved a wise counsellor to [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) who that autumn joined his Regiment, [The Kings Own Yorkshire Light Infantry](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King's_Own_Yorkshire_Light_Infantry) at [Malta](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malta) - accompanied by many prayers.*

*In 1886 the living of [Tattingstone](Tattingstone%20Rectory%2C%20Suffolk.md) became vacant, and was offered to Father by Aunt [Mary Elliott](Catherine%20Mary%20Pearson%20%28b.1815%29.md). (It had been offered him once before by my Father). In declining it - as he did not feel justified in leaving Cambridge - he suggested your Uncle [Jack](Rev.%20John%20Brattle%20Cane%20%281829-1896%29.md), as the most suitable person having married your Grandfather Elliott's [eldest daughter](Alicia%20Elliott%20%281832-1912%29.md); and in August they moved from [Weston](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weston,_Nottinghamshire), where they had spent 24 years, and enjoyed a very happy ten years in this dear old Home.*

*We went to [Keswick](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keswick,_Cumbria) ourselves that July* [1886] *- and were afterwards at [Ambleside](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambleside) with dear [Aunt Rick](Josephina%20Rickman%20%281808-1892%29.md) and [Alice](Alice%20Matilda%20Barton%20%28b.1862%29.md) for a spell; and spent August at [Barden](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barden,_Craven) on the [Wharf](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Wharfe), near Ilkley where the [Caustons](Josephina%20Barton%20%28b.1840%29.md) and we met again, as at Colwyn Bay, for several outings. Yorkshire is beautiful, and the [River Wharf](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Wharfe) is an ideal stormy rushing torrent; parts of it (where shallow) of that golden russet colour in which trout love to disport themselves. And the '[Strid](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Wharfe#The_Strid)', a romantic formation of rocks on this River, is said to have been leapt by a certain English notable when pursued. Nevertheless Barden was not a success; for we had a trying S.W. wind blowing all August; and I know I was sad about [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md), then a dreamy, sensitive lad of 17 years whose long legs looked too weak to carry him, and the burdens of his morbid and over-furtive imagination.*

*June 20 1887 was our beloved [Queen Victoria](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_victoria)'s [Jubilee Anniversary](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_victoria#Diamond_Jubilee), and we had great illuminations and decorations in Cambridge, though we did not go to London. In July Father and I went to Lowestoft as I had been ill, and took rooms near Uncle [Gerard](Rev.%20Gerard%20Barton%20%281834-1889%29.md), whom I then saw for the last time. [Madge](Madge%20Barton%20%28b.1869%29.md) sat by him shampooing his poor paralized hands and legs to ease him. He was only 52 but had suffered from 'creeping palsey' for 7 years. His was a philosophical and reverent mind; he was a deep student of theology, and something of a mystic. He took a very unusual step in being ordained at 40 by the Bishop of Norwich, after having farmed his own land at Fundenhall, Norfolk for years; and acted as Chaplain to his tenants there as a layman, no clergyman being resident there. He had a very steady eye,* **{p95}<a name="95"></a>** *and quiet restrained manner; and impressed you always with the idea that he left unsaid a good deal more than he said of what was in his mind on any given subject.*

*In August* [1887] *Father took [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) with him to Norway; and [Ernest Causton](Ernest%20Augustus%20Causton%20%28b.1867%29.md) accompanied them. They greatly enjoyed this trip for a month, and were charmed with the simplicity of the people, and the beauty of the fjords.*

*The following year (1888) we spent August again at* [Ambleside](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambleside)*; and took Mrs. Cousins' lodgings, where Father had been with [Aunt Rick](Josephina%20Rickman%20%281808-1892%29.md) in 1858. [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) came home from Malta that summer, for three months, and was with us again in the Lake District. He loved the dear old haunts, though he did not enjoy walking as much as the other brothers with Father over 'High Street', '[Scafell](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scafell)', '[Langdale Pikes](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Langdale_Pikes#The_Langdale_Pikes)', '[Fairfield](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairfield_(Lake_District))', '[Loughrigg](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loughrigg_Fell)', '[Bofell](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowfell)' and other mountains. During the three years of Arthur's absence he had been through the Burmese Campaign; and came home, with others, to get his medal, and returned in October to India, joined the [Bombay Rifles](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/125th_Napier's_Rifles), and worked there for an appointment in the Staff Corps. We did not see him again until 1896.*

*In March 1889 Father was asked if he would accept the Bishopric of Travancore; but declined. July 1889 we were again at Keswick; this time with [Aunt Rick](Josephina%20Rickman%20%281808-1892%29.md) and Father's sisters at Mrs. Petits', close to St. John's Church. The sunset view from the churchyard on a summer evening once seen can never be forgotten. [Derwent Water](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derwent_Water) in the foreground at your feet; '[Catbells](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catbells)' opposite - [Portinscale](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portinscale) in the distance, and [Saddleback](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blencathra) on the right. In August we crossed the water to [Peel](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peel,_Isle_of_Man) in the Isle of Man; and spent a very pleasant month with [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md); who had been working there a year with Bishop Bardsley as a layman, for [Seamen's Mission](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mission_to_Seafarers), since taking his B.A. degree, and before he went to [Ridley Hall](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridley_Hall,_Cambridge) to read for ordination.*

*Carol brought the four younger ones direct to Peel from Cambridge; and I think [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) and [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) cycled to Barrow and crossed thence to the Island; or that Cecil was in Northern Ireland that summer with John Robinson of Christ College and only Fred came to Peel. [Alice](Alice%20Matilda%20Barton%20%28b.1862%29.md) was with us also this month; and it was here that we received the sad news of her brother [Cam](John%20Campbell%20Barton%20%281858-1889%29.md)'s death in Sierra Leone. This was a heavy blow in every way to his good Father (Uncle [Joseph](Rev.%20Joseph%20Barton%20%281829-1905%29.md)) who lent much on his eldest son; and looked to him to father the four young step brothers and sisters when he should himself be* **{p96}<a name="96"></a>** *taken. Dear 'Cam' was most genuinely mourned by the young cousins, as he deserved; and they can never forget his unselfish devotion to them all.*

*The [Isle of Man](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Man) has a unique history all its own, with its [Parliament](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tynwald), and '[Deempster](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deemster)' and [Governor](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_the_Isle_of_Man); and its amusing conceit in speaking of "the adjacent Isles of England and Ireland"! [Peel Castle](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peel_Castle) is of historic interest since [Walter Scott](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Scott) made it known in one of his [standard works](http://www.archive.org/details/peverilpeak00unkngoog), and we went over its ruins, which stand out picturesquely on a narrow promontory. One long day we drove to [Erin Bay](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Erin) near which [King William's School](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_William's_College) stands; and whence Dean Farrar laid the scene of his story '[Eric](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric,_or,_Little_by_Little)'.*

*In October 1889 the dear Father promised to go out again to India for a year for C.M.S. They wanted him to accept the [Bishopric of Tinnevelly](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishop_of_tinnevelly) vacated by Bishop Sargent's death; but he preferred to take the work for a year without the Office; and the [Bishop of Ely](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishop_of_Ely) gave him leave of absence for this purpose. His aim was to make the Native Christian Church support and govern itself; by gradually withdrawing English money - and some of the Missionaries. He was kept in wonderful health, throughout a year of very arduous work in the tropics, and greatly enjoyed it.*

*Meanwhile the family dispersed; and Mr. Ireland-Jones went to [Trinity Vicarage, Cambridge](Holy%20Trinity%20Church%2C%20Cambridge.md#Brookside) as [Locum Tenens](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Locum_Tenens). [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md), [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) and I had rooms for six months in [Montpellier Road, Brighton](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?hl=en&q=Montpelier+Road,+Brighton&ie=UTF8&hq=&hnear=Montpelier+Rd,+Brighton,+East+Sussex+BN1,+United+Kingdom&gl=uk&ei=0Y0ETcGwF9yShAeGluTsBw&oi=geocode_result&ved=0CBoQ8gEwAA&z=16), and they continued their studies – attending Cambridge Extension Lectures - and had singing lessons of Madame Parrisotte, and pianoforte ditto. [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) went to [Ridley Hall](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridley_Hall,_Cambridge) this term, and whilst spending his Christmas holidays with me at Brighton became acquainted with Mr. Pearson, of St. Margaret's Church (which we attended) - and in January Mr. Pearson offered Jack a 'title' as soon as he should be ordained at Trinity 1890; and this resulted in two happy years work together. [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) and [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) were both at College now; at [St. John's College](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_John's_College,_Cambridge) and [Trinity Hall](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Hall,_Cambridge) respectively. [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) had just started school life at [Repton](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repton_School); whilst [Will](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) remained at the [Leys School Cambridge](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Leys_School) until September 1890 when he joined Guy at school.*

*[Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) was ordained on May 28 1890 at [Chichester Cathedral](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chichester_Cathedral), and we had quite a nice family gathering at the Old Hotel there for the Sunday - of Aunts and Uncle [Joseph](Rev.%20Joseph%20Barton%20%281829-1905%29.md), and the girls and myself - arranged by dear [Aunt Rick](Josephina%20Rickman%20%281808-1892%29.md), with special care because your dear Father could not be present. I think too that our Jack was the first of her great-nephews to be ordained. That* **{p97}<a name="97"></a>** *Sunday evening I went with Aunt [Bessie](Elizabeth%20Barton%20%28b.1832%29.md) to see the house built on the old Chichester Wall where your Grandfather Barton spent his last few years, and died. The following Sunday Jack preached his first sermon at [Tattingstone](Tattingstone%20Rectory%2C%20Suffolk.md) on [Phil. IV. 6,7](http://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Philippians#Chapter_4); and we spent a lovely week there with the dear [Canes](Rev.%20John%20Brattle%20Cane%20%281829-1896%29.md); and thence crossed to the Continent - Jack, [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md), [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) and myself; visiting first [Coblentz](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koblenz) on the Rhine, which I had not seen since my school days in 1855; and down the Rhine and saw [Heidelberg](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heidelberg), and its famous [Castle](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heidelberg_Castle) commanding the fine [River Neckar](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neckar). Thence we went to the [Black Forest](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Forest), stopping first at [Triberg](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triberg), and then at [Titisee](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titisee) during the three summer months.*

*In August* [1890] *[Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) left us to start work as a curate in Brighton - and he was at once replaced by [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md), [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) and [Will](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md). [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) was not of our party this summer, as he went, by invitation of a Cambridge party, with Frank Webb-Peploe to Mr. Moody's [Conference](http://www.biblebelievers.com/moody/15.html) at 'Northfields' America, to represent the [Cambridge Inter-Collegiate Christian Union](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambridge_Inter-Collegiate_Christian_Union); and after this interesting gathering of young men, they saw the Niagara Falls, and Lake Ontario. Returning home however, Fred wrote to us that England looked to his eyes - as he travelled on landing, from Liverpool to Devonshire, to join 'the Aunts' - like a prolonged and lovely garden, after the vast and bleak stretches of the great Western Continent.*

*This August* [1890] *was a very lively month at* [Titisee](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titisee)*; and the morning hour spent by our own Boys, and some others, in the Lake daily — bathing, diving, swimming etc. seemed to provide great amusement for the German onlookers. The girls got bathing also; and delightful walks in the surrounding pine woods, and on the Hills. Picnics with German and English friends whom we met in the hotel ('[Schwartzwald](http://www.schwarzwaldhotel-trescher.de/)') were great fun. The young people picked dishes of wild strawberries, and raspberries and bilberries in turn; and an old woman in a Chalet Farm near, gladly provided us with a pint bottle of cream for 3d. to add to the afternoon feast.*

*As we sat one morning at breakfast under the trees an Indian postcard was brought to me, addressed simply "Titisee - Switzerland"! It was from [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) telling us of his engagement to [Lilian Wyatt](Lilian%20Wyatt%20%28b.1870%29.md), daughter of our S.P.G. Missionary friend, and grand-daughter of the* **{p98}<a name="98"></a>** *excellent [Bishop Caldwell](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Caldwell), of Tinnevelly. Father and he had met the family at [Kodaikanal](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kodaikanal) in the [Palni Hills](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palni_Hills) - and I believe they were engaged a month after. We received much fuller news of course by the following mail, but this was my first intimation of the first daughter-in-law to be.*

*The month soon sped by; and we descended by a mountain railway to [Freiburg](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freiburg_im_Breisgau); where, at Pension* *Utz* [a guest house]*, we found those who had known Uncle [Gerard](Rev.%20Gerard%20Barton%20%281834-1889%29.md) and his family; who had resided there during three years, Uncle Gerard acting English Chaplain - and [Conrad](Conrad%20Barton%20%281879-1979%29.md) being born there.*

*About September 9th* [1890] *at 11 p.m. we saw [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) and [Will](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) off by train alone to England, via the [Rhine](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine) and [Rotterdam](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam); and a welcome awaited them at [Tattingstone](Tattingstone%20Rectory%2C%20Suffolk.md) from the dear [Uncle](Rev.%20John%20Brattle%20Cane%20%281829-1896%29.md) and [Aunt](Alicia%20Elliott%20%281832-1912%29.md) before the Repton term commenced. I have heard since that I was considered foolhardy, and even heartless to send these two boys aged 13 and nearly 15 a two days and two nights journey alone in a foreign land, the language of which they knew but very imperfectly. My experience however has proved that in order to make young folk self-reliant you must trust them early to 'go alone' - boys at least - and though Guy and Will had learnt up many German sentences, they found that they were generally answered in English, and they happily met with no mishaps.*

*Our reduced party, [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md), the [two](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) [girls](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) and I, now made our way to [Lucerne](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucerne), and thence to [Vitznau](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitznau), at the end of the Lake. This summer I again took up painting, after not having touched a brush for seven years. The beautiful bilberry that carpeted the ground at [Titisee](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titisee); a short, large, white thistle; and the hairy blue gentian and some others were the result of a few hours when Cecil and the sisters went long excursions - up [Mont Pilatus](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Pilatus), the [Rigi](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigi) etc. which I preferred not to join.*

*On a brilliant Sunday morning - September 28th* [1890] *- a telegram arrived from Father dated Brindisi or Boulogna saying that he was once more safe in Europe, and asking us to meet him next day at [Como](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Como). With glad hearts we crossed the Lake that morning to join in the nearest English service (which was at Lucerne); and the words of our beautiful Thanksgiving had a special meaning to our quartett - that we might "shew forth our praise, not with our lips only but also in our lives." Next morning at 6 a.m. we left* *[Vitznau](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitznau)* *by steamer for [Fluelen](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluelen), and ascended the [St. Gothard](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Gotthard_Pass) by train; and much enjoyed the wonderful views on that glorious pass - enhanced by the autumn coloring and brilliant sunshine. We parted with [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) at [Goeschenen](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%B6schenen), who was* **{p99}<a name="99"></a>** *going to walk thence to see the [Devil's Bridge](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teufelsbr%C3%BCcke), and return to Lucerne, and take train to England that night to keep his Cambridge Term.*

*Our train steamed into Como Station about 4 o'clock that afternoon – and there stood the dear Father, in his white solar helmet hat - we had seen no one who could compare with him for 12 months past - in our eyes. We took steamer to [Cadenabbia](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadenabbia); and spent ten very happy days at the "Hotel Belle Ile". The azaleas in the [Villa Carlotta](http://www.villacarlotta.it/sito/index2.php) were only less beautiful in September, than we found them six years later, when we were again there, May 1896 with [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) and [Lilian](Lilian%20Wyatt%20%28b.1870%29.md). The charm of Italian colouring, and the flower scented air, and dreamy, graceful movement of the people who blossom out in the '[dolce far niente](http://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/dolce_far_niente)' life cannot be reproduced in pen and ink; but we found that these combined with life on a lakeside, make an ideal Rest by the way. Thence we went for a few days to [Baveno](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baveno), on [Lake Maggiore](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lago_Maggiore), where the girls found that green figs ripened in an Italian sunshine were very different from the best to be procured in our little Island.*

*On October 8 or 9* [1890] *we set our faces Homewards - and spent the 12th (which was the 27th Anniversary of our Wedding Day) at [Coblentz](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koblenz) on the Rhine. At [Dusseldorf](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dusseldorf) we left [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) for eight months with the Gericke family to learn German; which proved to be both a happy and successful venture. We reached the dear Aunts' home at [Croydon](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croydon) with [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) on the 15th; and were welcomed in Cambridge again on the 17th having been absent a year and a day.*

# Generations come and go, 1891-1894

*In January 1891 the first marriage in the family took place - between our [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) and [Lilian Wyatt](Lilian%20Wyatt%20%28b.1870%29.md), on the South India Hills. After their honeymoon they settled for some months in [Bombay](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mumbai) - and then to [Shalabagh](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalabagh), North India; whence on September 27 we received a telegram saying that Arthur had been elected into the Punjab Commission, in which he can serve till he is 55; and hold the same appointment as [I.C.S.](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Civil_Service) men - and draw the same pay; the only difference between 'Military Civilians' and Indian Civil Service men being that on retiring the former draw the pension of the Military rank then due to them, whereas the I.C.S. draw £1000 a year, after 25 years service.*

*This summer* [1891] *some of us shared [Hawthorne Villa, Ambleside](http://www.lakelovers.co.uk/cs/properties/797#) with Aunt [Annie](Anne%20Barton%20%281831-1921%29.md) and the sisters, while the [Caustons](Rev.%20Tom%20Causton%20%281835-1899%29.md) had Leighton Lodge a stones throw* **{p100}**<a name="100"></a> *off. It was a wet August; nevertheless we had large gatherings of young people around us - the Ormsbys two doors off - and the [George Thorntons](Rev.%20George%20Ruthven%20Thornton%20%281845-1895%29.md) not far away. There were so many songsters among us that [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) got up a concert in the Village Schoolroom, and invited all the friends we knew in the place for an hour's music. The [Caustons](Rev.%20Tom%20Causton%20%281835-1899%29.md), [Bob Elliott](William%20Robert%20Elliott%20%28b.1871%29.md), and some of our own party formed a good sized Choral Society! and the Secular and Sacred Parts – which they kept separate, included many fine songs in harmony; such as Part I 'Blow gentle gales' - 'Have you seen my Flora' - Mendelssohn's Open Air Quartetts – and a 'Slumber Song' of [Claude Barton](Claude%20Barton%20%281866-1889%29.md)'s (son of Uncle [Gerard](Rev.%20Gerard%20Barton%20%281834-1889%29.md), and brother of [Madge](Madge%20Barton%20%28b.1869%29.md), of [Frank](Frank%20Rickman%20Barton%20%281865-1947%29.md), and [Hugh](Hugh%20Barton%20%28b.1867%29.md), and [Conrad](Conrad%20Barton%20%281879-1979%29.md)). In Part II we had 'Hear my prayer' and 'Lift thine eyes' - Mendelssohn; 'As pants the hart' Spohr - 'There is a green Hill' by Lord Henry Somerset, 'God is a Spirit' by Sterndale Bennett, and others.*

*In May 1892 the first great break came to us all; and the beloved '[Aunt Rick](Josephina%20Rickman%20%281808-1892%29.md)', who had 'mothered' eight of the Barton family, and 'grand-mothered' 44 of the rising generation received her call Home, and entered into her rest, aged 84. This rushing 20th Century will see no more of her calm dignified type we fear. You always felt in a cultured presence when with her; and that she was one whose chief life was lived in an atmosphere above this passing world; although she had no religious phraseology. Her sound judgment, unswayed by her heart, was such that every member of the family was guided by her more or less. She united a firm hand over her household with the gentlest manner, which made her truly an ideal Lady 'Mother'. Her servants adored her; and it was said they never left her but to marry or die. Dear old 'Wrapson' - whom she pensioned - lived 52 years in the family; and died about 1898 - aged I think 67.*

*She loved to talk of your [Grandfather Barton](John%20Barton%20Senior%20%281789-1852%29.md) – her only sister's Husband - to whom she felt she owed much of her education; having lived all his married life with him, and his widowed life also; and lived on after his death with his children by his request. She travelled abroad with him and her sister, when she was 20, in the days of Post chaises, and 'vetturinos', through France and Italy; and was his companion out riding when they settled in Hants, being a good horse-woman.* **{p101}<a name="101"></a>** *Before Railways intersected, and bisected the country and the Penny Post came in, and before Electric wires circumnavigated the world in 3½ minutes, as now, driving this generation to live at a pace which bids fair to annihilate all leisure, and all time for meditation, this Brother and Sister-in-law had time to read and digest books of which present day folk have only time to read Reviews.*

*When this noble, queenly and much loved lady 'crossed the Bar' she seemed like a ship in full sail going into port, 'into the haven where she would be'; and none could wish to keep her, although her place in our circle can never more be filled. [Father](Rev.%20John%20Barton%20of%20Cambridge%20%281836-1908%29.md), [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) and [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) went to Croydon to be present at the funeral at [Shirley](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirley,_London); and that afternoon the electric wires from the Far East brought news of the birth of our first grandchild - her first great-grand-niece - [Doris](Doris%20Barton%20%281892-1935%29.md) - our Arthur's daughter, born May 21.*\
*Uncle [Handley Moule](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) wrote the sweet lines which I append below, about the strange concurrence of these two events:*

*"RIDLEY HALL,* *CAMBRIDGE.* *May 23. 1892.*\
*My dearest Millie,*\
*I was thinking this afternoon of what I said to you on Sunday, about the sunset sky and the morning star, and in a quiet time in the 'Roundabout' I put the thought into verse (as overleaf), as a little message to you and dear John.*\
*Yr. loving Brother*\
*H.G.M.*

*May 21. 1892*\
*At noon your mother-friend you laid*\
*Beneath the flowers to rest;*\
*At eve the lightning-message play'd*\
*From India to the West;*\
*And grief forgot itself, and smiled*\
*To hail your children's new-born child;*\
*As if, at shut of summer day,*\
*When all its joys were done,*\
*The star of morning lit its ray*\
*Above the fallen sun,*\
*And lo, upon the darkling plain*\
*The golden dawn came up again."*

*In July* [1892] *we went to Red Hill, and occupied Mrs. McPherson's house for a fortnight - and here our [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md), on a Sunday, kept her 21st birthday. In August, Father took her and [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) to Norway - and the Stewards of Ipswich joined the party. I cannot describe their experiences as I was not with* **{p102}<a name="102"></a>** *them.*

*In September* [1892] *dear [Emmie Cane](Mary%20Emily%20Cane%20%28b.1866%29.md) was married at [Tattingstone Rectory](Tattingstone%20Rectory%2C%20Suffolk.md) to Douglas Hamilton, and went with him to Hong-Kong. I see have forgotten to mention [Nellie](Helen%20Isabel%20Cane%20%28b.1871%29.md)'s marriage to Frank Paynter in June 1891. The dear old Home and Church looked their best on both occasions, though parents must always hear minor chords in a peel of Wedding Bells.*

*On Trinity Sunday* [28 May?] *1893 our [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) was ordained at [St. Paul’s Cathedral](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Paul's_Cathedral) by [Bishop Temple](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Temple), and Father and I and he and [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) spent a few days at Holland Park, with Great Aunt [Mary Elliott](Catherine%20Mary%20Pearson%20%28b.1815%29.md). He was 'ordained to the Colonies'; and was to sail for India in October. We spent part of July and August at Keswick and Buttermere. We had rooms first at Mrs. Wilson's, Borrodaile, whence Father and I went to Keswick for the usual Conference; and we managed a house at the 'Heads' for 17 Missionaries, and our young people rowed in from Borrodaile for the morning meeting. There were some dear Saints at this gathering whom we were never to meet again on earth. Bishop Hill and his wife from Sierra Leone; who both died of fever the following January within a week of returning to their labours in West Africa, together with four others who all fell victims to that deadly climate, and were 'promoted' to higher service above. Cecil slept under canvas for several nights with the Bishop, whilst some special Missionary talks were being given; and he joined us in picnics more than once, and figures in a photo group taken by Jack this month. The Robert Stuarts also were two out of seven massacred on August 1 1895 at Kuchong by the Chinese '[Boxers](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxer_Rebellion)' - 'of whom the world was not worthy'.*

*In August* [1893] *we went over the [Honister Pass](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honister_Pass), to [Buttermere](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buttermere,_Cumbria) Vicarage, into which nutshell of a house six of us packed; while [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) and [Will](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md), with [Seymour Horan](Seymour%20Horan.md), had rooms out. Father was responsible for the duty there this month, in the very tiny Church; and it was a moving sight for me to see him assisted in the service by his two sons in Holy Orders. Aunt [Emily](Emily%20Barton%20%281842-1927%29.md) and [Alice](Alice%20Matilda%20Barton%20%28b.1862%29.md) put up at the hotel at Buttermere for a week, and thus enlarged our family party; and we had Tea picnics at [Crummock Water](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crummock_Water), and elsewhere; and all-day walking excursions. Ever since this summer Seymour has been more or less a member of the family, and quasi-brother to you all and my 'seventh son'; and as I write, we are within a few days of his entering the family as a Nephew.* **{p103}<a name="103"></a>**

*When we returned to Cambridge in September* [1893]*, [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md)'s last packings for India were done, and October was soon upon us. October 12 this year was the 30th anniversary of our Wedding Day, and Cecil's last day in England. It was a day of great thanksgiving, though shadowed by the prospect of parting for some years with Cecil. He had a few young friends in to spend the evening and to accentuate the festive, rather than the sad side of the day - and before parting they made a semi-circle round us, and held hands and joined in the chorus which Cecil played, of '[The Old Dutch](http://londonbobby.com/lblyric.htm#dutch)' -* *'We've lived together, love, for 30 years and it ain't been a day too much!'*

*The morning of the 13th rose grey and sad, but we were all resolved to be brave, and our dear [Boy](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) wrote in my Daily Light before he left 'He shall doubtless come again with joy'. It was a hopeful promise indeed wherewith to part; and as I gave him a last embrace in the boudoir at [Trinity Vicarage](Holy%20Trinity%20Church%2C%20Cambridge.md#Brookside), Rutherford's words recurred to me as an inspiring thought 'Lord - I do this for Thee'. Father and Jessie went to the station; and after ¼ hour alone, I went upstairs to comfort Ethel to whom this parting was the greatest sorrow her young life had known. We both realized that we should never again see him as he left us. Time must, and does bring changes. [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) accompanied Cecil to Liverpool, whence he sailed in a Hall Line steamer to [Karachi](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi), India, and thence by train to [Multan](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multan). Fred described the experience as one he would not care to repeat - and many have felt the same after seeing dear ones off in a steamer to the Far East, and watching it drift inch by inch from the pier, and steam slowly down-stream until divided indeed by the great waters - 'It must be for years, and it may be for ever'.*

*This second great break of a son going to India was to be a crisis, and ushered in the closing chapter of our Cambridge life. For soon after this parting your Father was offered simultaneously the Secretaryship of the [Church Pastoral Aid Society](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Pastoral_Aid_Society), and a Bishopric in Japan. He accepted the former, considering that as he was in his 57th year, a younger man than himself might do better work in Cambridge than he after 16½ years of strenuous labour for both the University and Town; and that he was too old for the latter.* **{p104}<a name="104"></a>**

*I have often since had reason to think that this giving up of so important a position (where in addition to all other work he had been Rural Dean for two years) was a climax of the unworldliness for which his life had been conspicuous. Especially as by espousing an unpopular cause and Society, he burnt behind him the bridge to any Ecclesiastical preferment. But such an idea would have no weight with him; and he took up the new work keenly because he realized that he could encourage and strengthen the hands of the Evangelical clergy all over England; and the five years which followed proved that the 500 whom he visited in their own parishes during that time, looked upon him as a true Father in God.*

*The breaking up of the Home we had loved so long was sad; and [Trinity Church](Holy%20Trinity%20Church%2C%20Cambridge.md), with all its interesting associations; and leaving the [Moules](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) behind and all connected with [Ridley Hall](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridley_Hall,_Cambridge), and the University, was a great wrench; but our path of duty seemed clear. Our dear people gave us the beautiful Hall Clock with Cambridge chimes which you know; and large framed pictures of Trinity Church inside and out, and of the adjoining [Henry Martyn Hall](http://131.111.227.198/CHall.htm) - (which Uncle [Handley](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) once said ought to be called 'the Barton Memorial' instead of 'Martyn Memorial'! The young people, sons and daughters of our tradespeople, who had for many years attended my Vicarage Sunday Bible Classes, gave me a box of plated Fish knives and forks. We left Cambridge the middle of December* [1893]*; and spent Christmas at [Croydon](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croydon) – some at [Thornhaugh](Thornhaugh%2C%20Croydon.md), others at Powyscout.*

# Wimbledon, 1894-1899
