*On February 1 1894 our furniture began to be moved to the new home we had selected at [Wimbledon](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wimbledon,_London); and as I passed from one deserted room to another at [Trinity Vicarage](Holy%20Trinity%20Church%2C%20Cambridge.md#Brookside), having seen the last van off which contained our household gods, I realized that it is not walls of bricks and mortar which make 'Home' - but those whom you live with and who are in close affinity with you; and the furniture and pictures etc. you have surrounded yourself with, and become attached to during the course of years.*

*Sarah Phillips came with us from Cambridge, and has been first housemaid, and now parlourmaid ever since; and has served us devotedly and faithfully 10 years.* [Footnote by [JEBB](Lt.%20Col.%20Ted%20Barton%20%281897-1971%29.md): *And another 51 years after that, until the death of [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) in 1955.*] **{p105}<a name="105"></a>**

*We named our new Home on Wimbledon Hill 'Castelnau'* [Footnote by [JEBB](Lt.%20Col.%20Ted%20Barton%20%281897-1971%29.md): *Now Junior House of [Wimbledon High School](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wimbledon_High_School) for Girls*]*, after the [Boileau ancestors](Antoine%20Boileau%20%28d.1530%29.md)' [Chateau](Chateau%20Castelnau.md) near [Nismes](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C3%AEmes), South France; and found several old friends settled there before us. Col. Henry S. Clarke, and family, my brother [Arthur](Arthur%20Wilmot%20Elliott%20%281841-1862%29.md)'s [Addiscombe College](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Addiscombe_Military_Academy) friend, owned a house at Wimbledon, and happened to be Treasurer to the [C.P.A.S.](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Pastoral_Aid_Society) and he went daily to Town, and worked in the same office in [Falcon Court, Fleet Street](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?q=Falcon+Ct,+City+of+London,+London+EC4Y+1BW,+United+Kingdom&ie=UTF8&hl=en&cd=1&geocode=FUYKEgMdxFL-_w&split=0&sll=53.800651,-4.064941&sspn=6.881357,14.941406&hq=&hnear=Falcon+Ct,+London+EC4Y+1BW,+United+Kingdom&ll=51.513851,-0.110067&spn=0.000921,0.002411&z=19), E.C. The Francis Foxes, she née [Selina Wright](http://www.thepeerage.com/p36536.htm#i365351) of [Osmaston Manor](_Memoirs%20of%20John%20Barton.md#osmaston-manor-1856), invited us to spend our first week with them, until our furniture was settled in. The Douglas Foxes, brother of Francis, who had married [Mary Wright](http://www.thepeerage.com/p36506.htm#i365060), lived at Coombe Springs two miles off. And we attended the ministry of an old friend, Mr. Edward Moore, at Emmanuel Church.*

*[Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) was now 'walking the hospitals' at [St. Thomas'](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Thomas'_Hospital), London, and lived at home, going occasionally to Cambridge to pass an [M.B.](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor_of_Medicine) examination; and [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) had begun his Cambridge course, and was settled at [Pembroke College](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pembroke_College,_Cambridge). He inclined at this time to take up engineering, and as he is a good draughtsman, and good at mathematics we thought these gifts pointed to this profession. However, after having pursued these studies for two years, he told us that he had no real drawing to the work, and that Medicine and Anatomy attracted him far more. Dear Father made no difficulty about this 'family bomb-shell', as some of you called it, and only said that it was well that Guy had discovered his bias toward the Medical profession at 20 instead of at 30!*

*Early in June* [1894] *dear Father fulfilled a long promise, and took me to [Torquay](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torquay) and [Babbicombe](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babbacombe), old haunts of his in 1860, but which I had never seen. The views of coast and sea, and the flowers, that delight to grow in that soft air make it a veritable [English Riviera](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Riviera). At Babbicombe we met the Augustus Tollemaches, old friends of [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md)'s; and he took us a climb up a Devonshire lane to see [Lady Mount Temple](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Cowper-Temple,_1st_Baron_Mount_Temple#Personal_life)'s romantic home 'Cliff House'; where with their friend Mr. Clifford (the artist's) help, they had suitable quotations carved round the frieze of each room to suit its aspect, and distant outlook on the beautiful surrounding country.*

*We had felt doubtful whether we could manage a summer holiday this year with the family; but eventually decided that as Father was anxious to visit the Welsh clergy assisted by grants of money from the [C.P.A.S.](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Pastoral_Aid_Society), Wales should be our destination for August.* **{p106}<a name="106"></a>**

*In May* [1894] *I had found myself seated at the C.M.S. Annual Meeting, at Exeter Hall, behind Mr. [H. E.Thornton](Henry%20Edward%20Thornton%20%281842-1926%29.md), and spoke to him for the first time though we had known and loved his mother, Mrs. [Spencer Thornton](Rev.%20Spencer%20Ruthven%20Thornton%20%281813-1850%29.md), and sisters at [Broxbourne](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broxbourne) for years. Mr. Thornton asked me whether our son [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) was in the Hall? as he had often heard of but never seen him; and I pointed Jack out to his future Father-in-law! A week later the Father received one of Mr. H. E. T's amusing postcards saying "Swansea Vicarage is to let; and we are to be at the [Grenfells](Pascoe%20St.%20Leger%20Grenfell%20%281798-1874%29.md)' old home there en famille. Do come." And thus it came about that our two families were thrown together almost daily for a month. Six young Thorntons, and their cousin Grace [Trotter](Eleanor%20Catherine%20Grenfell%20%281846-1928%29.md) and five of ours swam dived and bathed, cycled walked picniced and sang together whilst we six elders, the Thorntons Trotters and ourselves, talked over many mutual interests. Amongst these the Welsh clergy were not forgotten, and Father convened a gathering of 40 of these for the day, and we entertained them at luncheon and tea at a Parish Hall in* *[Swansea](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swansea)* *before and after their discussions.*

*After this Father and I went for a tour through the [Rhonda Valley](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhondda) for 10 days with Archdeacon Griffiths as guide, and visited all the Welsh Clergy whose parishes were large enough to be aided by Church Pastoral Aid. This was an interesting experience, for we had never before seen such a large mining district, or heard of or seen anything of the lives lived by those to whom we owe the comfort that coal supplies. We stayed first at [Neath](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neath); then at [Ystradyfdog](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ystradyfodwg) where all mines are owned by [Mrs. Llewelyn](Madelina%20Georgina%20Grenfell%20%281826-1903%29.md), [Susie](Susan%20Thornton%20%281875-1958%29.md)'s aunt, and where she has finished building a Church begun by her husband who had died four years previously, for the thousands who live in and over his mines. They spent £25,000 on this beautiful Church, sparing no money to procure marbles and carving wherewith to embellish God's House. When complete Mrs. Llewelyn had a small brass plate let into the marble Chancel wall giving her husband's name and date of death etc. and underneath these words "Of Thine own have we given Thee".*

*A mining community, not unnaturally, seems to be unique both outwardly and inwardly, and the people differ widely from those of their own class whose lives are spent under other conditions. One day I was attracted* **{p107}<a name="107"></a>** *by a long procession of men, in black, who were at some distance coming along the side of a hill, and asked a Vicar who was shewing us round his parish whether it was a funeral party? 'Oh no!' he replied, 'only men coming home from night work in the mines to their day rest!' He added 'the beds are never cold here - one set of men occupy them by night, and another set by day!' Miners are very cleanly in their habits. Coming up from the Pits as black as sweeps, the suit of clothes worn below ground is thrown at once into a large empty box kept for the purpose, and every home is provided with a full length bath, fed by hot and cold water pipes, into which these coal-blackened men plunge, to emerge unrecognizably fair, ruddy and clean; and they are to be seen after a good sleep, standing in groups chatting together, dressed in immaculate light tweed suits, and generally scarlet ties. The Archdeacon told us that miners are great readers, and thinkers, and particularly fond of theological books and discussion. He had got a fine Library and Reading room built for them at [Ystradefadog](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ystradyfodwg), which was extensively used, and the men there had just asked him to spend £40 on their behalf in fresh books. Miners often work only four days a week, and take Saturday, Sunday and Monday for amusement.*

*Dissent is very prevalent in some places, and we passed 24 Chapels in one street three miles long, distinguished by Biblical names such as 'Salem', 'Bethesda', 'Bethany', 'Sion' etc., and music of various kinds is to be heard everywhere, either on flutes, guitars, banjos or cornets. Walking along the roads, or in the railway trains, they break into part singing as naturally as may be heard in Germany. These national songs are generally wild, weird, and minor, and the voices deep and melodious. On Mondays and Tuesdays everyone seems to be on the move; and we had quite a difficulty in getting seats in the trains on these days; men, women and children, all the working classes were travelling. Mothers usually wearing a shawl round their heads, cleverly carry a small child on the hip supported by this shawl, which eases the arm.*

*The Welsh clergy are not usually up to the standard socially of our English clerics, but are perhaps more easily fluent; and services are usually bi-lingual, Gaelic and English each having their due share.* **{p108}<a name="108"></a>** *On returning to [Swansea](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swansea) we spent a Sunday at [Dingestow Court](http://www.visionofbritain.org.uk/place/place_page.jsp?p_id=5193), the Bosanquet’s beautiful old home, whose two sons we had known at [Trinity College Cambridge](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_College,_Cambridge); one now doing exploration work in Egypt, the other at Odessa. The curtains and bed hangings of the room we occupied were the most beautiful tapestry designs and needlework I have seen in any private house; the work is sald to be 200 or 300 years old. We rowed down the lovely [River Wye](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Wye), after we left Dingestow, and went over [Tintern Abbey](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tintern_Abbey) on its banks; and shortly after this our party broke up at Swansea. [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) returned to his lonely life in [Sunderland](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunderland,_Tyne_and_Wear), with a warm remembrance of [Susie](Susan%20Thornton%20%281875-1958%29.md) in his mind and we promised that [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) should go and keep him company there for six months. This proved a useful and happy time; and Ethel started work among the women and girls connected with the [Seamen's Mission](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mission_to_Seafarers), which was continued by Susie a year later.*

*You four younger ones came with us to North Wales, via [Aberystwith](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aberystwyth), [Barmouth](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barmouth), [Festiniog](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blaenau_Ffestiniog), [Bettwys-y-Coed](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betws-y-Coed), and [Llanfairfechan](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llanfairfechan), whence you returned home; and Father and I stayed for another gathering of 40 or more Welsh Clergy. Thence to [Llandudno](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llandudno) and [Wrexham](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrexham) where we spent a day or two with Dean Howell; and saw a chained Bible in the Church 400 years Old. We walked with the Vicar of [Holywell](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holywell), near Wrexham, to see some famous warm springs, which are believed by the superstitious to have miraculous power; and around which hung many crutches and splints left behind as votive offerings by those who had been healed, which reminded us of similar scenes amongst Roman Catholics abroad. We stayed at [Chester](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester) with friends for a night, and saw the [Cathedral](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester_Cathedral)* [footnote by [JEBB](Lt.%20Col.%20Ted%20Barton%20%281897-1971%29.md): *where Ted and Molly were married in 1924*]*, and most picturesque old houses for which it is famous; and then found our way home to Wimbledon and settled down for the winter.*

*In September this year* [1894] *our [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) was very happily engaged at [Simla](Shimla%2C%20India.md) to the daughter of a very old friend, [Esther Broadbent](Esther%20Broadbent%20%281873-1959%29.md), child of [Col. Broadbent](Col.%20John%20Edward%20Broadbent%20%281845-1931%29.md) and of his wife, formerly [Dora Nicholson](Dora%20Nicholson%20%281844-1897%29.md) whom we had known intimately in* [Calcutta](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolkata) *between 1865 and 1870. Esther and her mother came home in November, and Esther soon found a place in our hearts after we met her at her uncle [Sir William Broadbent](Sir%20William%20Henry%20Broadbent%20%281835-1907%29.md)'s house in London.*

*[Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) came home for Christmas from [Sunderland](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunderland,_Tyne_and_Wear); and told us that [Mr. H. E. Thornton](Henry%20Edward%20Thornton%20%281842-1926%29.md) had paid them a visit, as he was interested in [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md)'s work;* **{p109}<a name="109"></a>** *and had volunteered to contribute half the salary of a lay reader to assist Jack as he thought he ought to have more help. He also gave Jack a warm invitation to take a week's rest in January after the very heavy Xmas and New Year's work; and spend it in their home at the [Ropewalk](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?f=q&source=s_q&hl=en&geocode=&q=58+the+ropewalk,+nottingham&sll=52.953844,-1.159362&sspn=0.007136,0.01929&gl=uk&ie=UTF8&hq=&hnear=58+The+Ropewalk,+Nottingham+NG1+5,+United+Kingdom&ll=52.95231,-1.157427&spn=0.007136,0.01929&z=16), Nottingham.*

*On January 11th 1895 dear Father and I returned late from a devotional meeting, where Parents had met specially to intercede for their families, and found lying on the Hall Table a letter addressed in [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md)'s neat handwriting, with the Nottingham postmark. As we took it into the Dining Room to read, we guessed the contents before breaking the seal! and soon found that our prayers for this dear Son's happiness had been answered. The Father sat up late to assure him of our sympathy and warm approval of his suit, and posted the letter before midnight. Next morning he also telegraphed, knowing that Jack must leave Nottingham and return that day to [Sunderland](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunderland,_Tyne_and_Wear) for Sunday duty; and soon we had happy letters telling us of his engagement to [Susie](Susan%20Thornton%20%281875-1958%29.md) - who has been a very dear daughter to us now for nine years.*

*In February 1895 both [Susie](Susan%20Thornton%20%281875-1958%29.md) and [Esther](Esther%20Broadbent%20%281873-1959%29.md), and their Mothers, and [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) met one day at Castelnau, Wimbledon, and were introduced to those of the family then at home.*\
*[Mr. Thornton](Henry%20Edward%20Thornton%20%281842-1926%29.md) objected to long engagements, and as soon as Susie passed her 20th birthday, arrangements were made for the wedding at Nottingham, and a happier one we never attended than her's and Jack's on July 31 1895. The gathering of the Clans is the best part of most of these functions, except for the young couple themselves; and where most are of one mind, and old friends or relatives, and where a fine summer day is secured, nothing can be more enjoyable. Your Father tied the knot - the Bride had six maids to follow her, two of them her own, and two Jack's sisters. The union with the Thornton family was entirely after our hearts, and we were deeply thankful that God had given our Jack, with his young wife, all the good things both for this life and for the next which we, as parents, had for years asked of Him. The large family dinner party after the bridal pair had gone off to [Rowsley](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rowsley), Derbyshire, was kept lively by stories from [Mrs. Thornton](Katherine%20Charlotte%20Grenfell%20%281842-1906%29.md)'s brother, now [Lord Grenfell](Lord%20Francis%20Wallace%20Grenfell%20%281840-1925%29.md) - who though not then himself a father, had infinite sympathy with, and love for young people; and we experienced that evening none of the proverbial 'depression' after the Bride and her Groom's departure!* **{p110}<a name="110"></a>**

*On August 1* [1895] *we all separated; and the Thorntons and our own party made our way respectively to [Keswick](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keswick,_Cumbria) and [Borrodaile](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borrodale). For a second time we took Mrs. Wilson's cottage at Lodore; and spent a very happy month. The four younger brothers and sisters spent every fine day either on or in the Lake - where much swimming and singing went on. By the middle of August [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) and [Su](Susan%20Thornton%20%281875-1958%29.md) joined us for a week, and we had some delightful picnics and trips together. [Hugh Barton](Hugh%20Barton%20%28b.1867%29.md) also paid us a short visit from Liverpool, where he then had a five year appointment with the Royal Engineers; and he left us to go to his sister [Chrissy](Chrissie%20Barton%20%28b.1874%29.md), whose husband Ashton Rathbone died shortly after at Malvern. It was then also that Hugh made the acquaintance of Polly Gedge whom he married 6 months later.*

*In September* [1895] *we settled down again at Wimbledon; and dear Father continued his devoted labours for the [Church Pastoral Aid Society](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Pastoral_Aid_Society), leaving home for his London office at 9 a.m. and seldom returning before 6 or 7 p.m. Three weeks out of four he spent from Friday to Tuesday away visiting clergy all over England in their own parishes; 500 of such during the five years he held this office. He has had a real passion for work all his life; and used to say at this time that his favourite recreation was a Railway journey.*

*It must have been this year that you dear Girls were asked to help among the upper class girls; a movement which had been started by Miss M. Pollock to induce those who had left school to use their Time and Talents for those poorer and less favoured than themselves. Work amongst factory girls in [Bermondsey](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bermondsey) was the result of this effort; and from about this time a settlement was supported by 'T. and T.' ladies where four or five honorary lady workers lived who devoted themselves to this work. Though begun with few, there are now (1906) from 400 to 500 Factory girls connected with and helped by this Club. [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) was Volunteer Secretary for the Wimbledon Branch of Time and Talents Girls; and I think joined to help in this work; and used to go up to the Settlement to assist in winter at the cheap dinners for girls in Bermondsey; and in summer sent up hundreds of bouquets of flowers to the Factories once a week; generally taking the hampers full themselves; two or three ladies going round with the flowers, and giving the poor Girls a five minutes helpful talk during the dinner hour - with permission of the Owner and Master - and ending with a hymn.* **{p111}<a name="111"></a>**

*The year 1896 was an eventful one in the family. In May [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) and [Lilian](Lilian%20Wyatt%20%28b.1870%29.md) and their three children* [[Doris](Doris%20Barton%20%281892-1935%29.md), [Vyvian](Vyvian%20Barton%20%28b.1893%29.md) and [Keith](Keith%20Barton%20%281896-1916%29.md)] *came home; the former for seven weeks only, after 7½ years absence; and his wife and children were introduced to us for the first time. My meeting with the dear Son was rather amusing, and gave occasion for a great joke against me. It so happened that when Arthur's telegram arrived from Paris saying that he would reach Victoria Station at 7.30 p.m., I was the only person at home to go and meet him. When the Dover mail train ran in I could see no one amongst the 80 or 90 passengers resembling the Arthur of '88 - so I asked a gentleman whom I saw unmolested by relations whether he could tell me if a Mr. Barton was on the train? He replied 'Yes - certainly' and went on hesitatingly 'I am Mr. Barton ... Why, is it? ... Yes! it is my own Mother!' - and she had not recognized him!! He was most amusingly 'at sea' after so long an absence from England, but ready to do anything suggested. He had sent his big luggage round by the Bay; with Lilian and the children and ayah; so had only handbags; and a hansom soon took us to Vauxhall Station; and a train thence to Wimbledon, where we found a happy party awaiting us, Father, [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md), [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md), [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md), also I think [Eda](Marion%20Edith%20Barton%20%28b.1871%29.md) or [Kathleen](Kathleen%20Barton%20%28b.1874%29.md) Barton* [footnote by [JEBB](Lt.%20Col.%20Ted%20Barton%20%281897-1971%29.md): *from whom J.E.B.B. inherited the [portrait](Emily%20Elliott%20%281839-1924%29.md#portrait%20of%20Arthur%20and%20Emily) of [Emily](Emily%20Elliott%20%281839-1924%29.md) and [Arthur](Arthur%20Wilmot%20Elliott%20%281841-1862%29.md)*]*. The Sisters had grown up during Arthur's absence, and he was much charmed to make their acquaintance!*

*Everything English is a pleasure to Anglo Indians; and the Saturday evening sirloin of beef awaiting us appeared to him 'such a fine size' that he could hardly eat his portion for admiration! and another day a leg of mutton was a 'picture'! It was indeed a very happy reunion - and the dear Son's only sorrow was that he had been so long away, that he had forgotten a good deal how unchangeable the Old Home hearts must ever be towards him. 'Never again will I be so long away' he said; and he has kept his word.*

*On May 4th* [1896] *the beloved 'Uncle [Jack](Rev.%20John%20Brattle%20Cane%20%281829-1896%29.md)' (Cane) went to Glory after a short illness; and [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) and I went to [Tattingstone](Tattingstone%20Rectory%2C%20Suffolk.md) to see him laid to rest there on my birthday the 7th. Within three months that sweet home was broken up; and dear Aunt [Alicia](Alicia%20Elliott%20%281832-1912%29.md) and the Twins had scattered. No one can fill that dear Uncle's place to any of you young ones; and his memory is a fragrant and beloved one. He was an ideal Father in his family, and a faithful* **{p112}<a name="112"></a>** *shepherd to his flock, and knew every child in the village by name. His sense of humour amounted almost to genius; and you dear boys loved to call him 'sportsmanlike'. Such as he was, and is, lives with us still - though we see him not.*

*On May 22nd* [1896] *[Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md)'s first-born was given to us all, and '[Jackie](Jackie%20Barton%20%281896-1904%29.md)' was a very special loan to the family for 8½ bright happy years - but God took him November 1904.*

*The end of June* [1896] *dear [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) had to return to India, his seven weeks holiday being over; and he left [Lilian](Lilian%20Wyatt%20%28b.1870%29.md) to follow him a year later.*

*We spent August of this year* [1896] *at [Grantown](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grantown-on-Spey) N.B.* [North of the border?]*, a quiet Highland resort where [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) and [Annie](Anne%20Stote%20Fox%20%281867-1957%29.md) joined us, also Marcia Rickard* [footnote by [JEBB](Lt.%20Col.%20Ted%20Barton%20%281897-1971%29.md): *Later married to Dr. Claudius, and mother of Agnes and Dorothy*]*, [Seymour Horan](Seymour%20Horan.md), and the four younger ones. [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) and Seymour had some fine cycling together in Scotland. The Wardlaw-Ramsays had a house near us too; and we often met, and had lovely excursions in the neighbourhood together - that to the [Loch an Eilan](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loch_an_Eilein) being the one most impressed on my mind's eye and memory. Your dear Father had not half the rest that was due to him this summer - because my Father's legacy (which had been tied up to accumulate for 21 years from the day of his death in 1875) was released from Chancery on July 1st and divided amongst you all, and had to be reinvested, which gave him a good deal of work.*

*On October 21* [1896] *[Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) and [Esther](Esther%20Broadbent%20%281873-1959%29.md) were married at [Murree](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murree), in India, by good Bishop [Matthew](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_James_Matthew)s of Lahore. [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) was alas! the only one to represent the family, and he sent us a delightful account of that happy and festive occasion. [Colonel](Col.%20John%20Edward%20Broadbent%20%281845-1931%29.md) and [Mrs. Broadbent](Dora%20Nicholson%20%281844-1897%29.md) grudged nothing to their sweet and only daughter - and the Mother (our friend Dora Nicholson of early* [Calcutta](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolkata) *days) quite accepted Cecil as a son, during the eight months she lived after this marriage - after which a sudden illness called her 'Home'* [footnote by [JEBB](Lt.%20Col.%20Ted%20Barton%20%281897-1971%29.md): *In June, 1897. Two months laters Col. Broadbent having gone to the Malakand Campaign as Chief Engineer, Esther travelled to Barnes Court, [Simla](Shimla%2C%20India.md), residence of '[Uncle Willy](Sir%20William%20Mackworth%20Young%20%28b.1840%29.md)' Mackworth Young, then Lieutenant-Governor of the Punjab, where [Ted](Lt.%20Col.%20Ted%20Barton%20%281897-1971%29.md) was born, on September 6th.*]*. After a brief honeymoon this young couple settled down in Multan to work there for two years.*

*On December 23rd* [1896] *[Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) and [Annie](Anne%20Stote%20Fox%20%281867-1957%29.md) followed their example and were married at Norbiton Church, near Kingston, on a memorably foggy and bitter winter's day. I remember Fred saying à propos the terrible weather as they started for their honeymoon at Davos, before he shut up the windows of the brougham, "We are very sorry for our friends, but the weather does not affect us in* **{p113}<a name="113"></a>** *the least"! And with this happy speech and radiant faces, they started on life's journey together - under circumstances uniquely favourable to Fred; who got his Practice, his Wife, and his new Home all in the same month at 28. Your Father and Bishop Tucker tied that knot.*

*The Easter of 1897 dear Father and I spent at [Penzance](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penzance), and [St. Ives](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Ives,_Cornwall), which we had not seen before; and [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md), [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) and [Will](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) followed us, cycling. On April 21 this year [Isa Young](Isabel%20Mary%20Young%20%281870-1929%29.md) married [Vere Monro](Archibald%20Vere%20Monro%20%281867-1944%29.md) at Government House, [Lahore](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore); and [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md), [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) and [Esther](Esther%20Broadbent%20%281873-1959%29.md) were able to represent this family. Dear Isa is one of the few in the world who is unselfish to a fault, as you all know; and Vere was a happy man to get such a wife.*

*The month of August* [1897] *found us again at [Ambleside](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambleside), at Mrs. Leighton's; and [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) and [Susie](Susan%20Thornton%20%281875-1958%29.md), and their [Jackie](Jackie%20Barton%20%281896-1904%29.md), now 15 months old were with us - also dear [George Barton](George%20Norman%20Barton%20%281876-1900%29.md). The [Canes](Rev.%20John%20Brattle%20Cane%20%281829-1896%29.md) too, were at Loughriggholme, and we often met and had picnics together.*

*I cannot recall anything special about this winter, except that dear Father was working at very high pressure for the [C.P.A.S.](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Pastoral_Aid_Society); and although he is drawn in one of Uncle [Handley](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md)'s books as the model of a 'moderate man', I know that his passion for work made him lack moderation in that particular. Quite lately (1905) he said "I am paying heavily now for overworking. [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) and [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) should take warning from me". He burnt the candle at both ends, and it burnt out fast in consequence.*

*The summer of 1898 saw the Father abroad in July alone - for a spell - though he met [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) at [Murren](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%BCrren), and the Athertons. [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) and [Annie](Anne%20Stote%20Fox%20%281867-1957%29.md) also went to Switzerland, and we had their little [Marjorie](Emily%20Marjory%20Barton%20%28b.1897%29.md), now 8 months, at 'Castelnau'. [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) was now living at home, which was a comfort to us, and 'walking the Hospitals' at [St.Thomas'](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Thomas'_Hospital). For August we went to [Eynsford Rectory, Kent](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?q=Eynsford+Rectory,+Kent&ie=UTF8&hl=en&hq=&hnear=The+Rectory,+Pollyhaugh,+Eynsford,+Dartford,+Kent+DA4+0HF,+United+Kingdom&z=16), and Father took the locum tenancy, but it was not much of a change, nor very exciting.*

*When autumn came we began to look forward to the return of [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) and his [wife](Esther%20Broadbent%20%281873-1959%29.md) and son [Ted](Lt.%20Col.%20Ted%20Barton%20%28b.1897%29.md) - and were thankful when they safely arrived on Sunday evening November 13th* [1898]*. Cecil's calm and Esther's pluck carried them through a long and anxious journey; and after six weeks quiet rest, dear Esther gave us twin-granddaughters, on December 27, who were christened in February 1899, and called '[Dora](Dora%20Barton%20%281898-1962%29.md) and [Joan](Joan%20Barton%20%281898-1900%29.md)'. They were a great joy to their dear Mother and me, though of course a great handful; and we were rejoiced to give them a home* **{p114}<a name="114"></a>** *for three months, especially as Esther's own dear [mother](Dora%20Nicholson%20%281844-1897%29.md) was gone aloft, and [Col. Broadbent](Col.%20John%20Edward%20Broadbent%20%281845-1931%29.md) had not then retired from Indian life, so they had no Home in England this year but ours. On leaving us they spent several weeks at [Boscombe](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boscombe) with their little family; and paid some visits until August, when they joined us at [Little Munden](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?f=q&source=s_q&hl=en&geocode=&q=Little+Munden&sll=51.849459,-0.021286&sspn=0.053974,0.154324&ie=UTF8&hq=&hnear=Little+Munden,+Hertfordshire,+United+Kingdom&ll=51.878716,-0.053558&spn=0.02697,0.077162&z=14) Rectory, near Ware, an ideal country home and garden, where Father took duty for two months for Mr. [Robert Monro](Rev.%20Robert%20Douglas%20Monro.md), who married an [Elliott cousin](Anna%20Maria%20Elliott%20%281837-1883%29.md) of mine.*

*His engagement as Secretary to the* *[C.P.A.S.](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Pastoral_Aid_Society)* *had come to an end at the New Year - and we had decided to winter in Italy as soon as the [Cecils](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) returned to India. And meanwhile we had a delightful summer gathering in this Hertfordshire home. Visits first from our [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md)'s trio and Nurse Ives, who had recently brought home [Vyvian](Vyvian%20Barton%20%28b.1893%29.md) and [Keith](Keith%20Barton%20%281896-1916%29.md) from India. Next from [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) and [Sue](Susan%20Thornton%20%281875-1958%29.md) and their [Jackie](Jackie%20Barton%20%281896-1904%29.md) and [Bernard](Bernard%20Cecil%20Leslie%20Barton%20%281898-1992%29.md). [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) cycled over once or twice from Wimbledon - and Cecil's party were with us a long month. It was here that [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) originated the idea of a Blackberry Service, at which all the village children brought offerings of fruit, which when made into jam was sold for the benefit of C.P.A.S.. Cecil preached a delight fully apt sermon on 'Brambles' - one lovely September afternoon* [1899]*, as the sun lit up and glorified all the autumn foliage with which the pulpit, as well as hampers and baskets of blackberries had been decorated. The sermon, was afterwards printed as a booklet, and widely used in following years for Blackberry services.*

# Travels in Southern Europe, 1899-1900

[[Embedded Google map]](https://maps.google.com/maps/ms?t=p&ie=UTF8&hl=en&msa=0&msid=113677220254684947542.00049747f9c7e4db860bb&ll=44.527843,8.876953&spn=9.301864,35.112305&z=5&output=embed)

*In October* [1899] *we let Castelnau for 18 months to the Cadells; and settled [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) and [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) in comfortable rooms in Spencer Road, Wimbledon, and [Father](Rev.%20John%20Barton%20of%20Cambridge%20%281836-1908%29.md), [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) and I started on our travels abroad on November 9th, the dear [Cecils](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) having preceded us, and sailed for India (where [Kashmir](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir) was their destination) on the 4th [inst.](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Latin_abbreviations#Less_common_abbreviations_and_usages).*

*During the next ten months, [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md)'s graphic pen described our travels in such interesting Circular letters to the family that I must refer you to them for details of all we saw and did in the South of France and in Italy; the Tyrol, and Switzerland; and give only a sketch of our delightful sojourn abroad until September 1900; when we returned to Old England; glad enough to be home again; but also deeply thankful for such health all the time that we never once had to call in a doctor; and rejoiced that we had been able to* **{p115}<a name="115"></a>** *enjoy the charms of Italian scenery, coloring, antiquities and language for once with you two dear Daughters.*

*Our first stage was to [Nimes](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nimes), where we spent three or four nights only, and made acquaintance with my French relative, the Marquis de Valfons of Castelnau, 15 miles beyond Nimes and to which we drove, and saw over the restored [Chateau](Chateau%20Castelnau.md) which has been 400 years the property of the [Boileau](Antoine%20Boileau%20%28d.1530%29.md) family. The present Marquis came to it through his mother, Gabrielle Boileau, who was a more direct heir than any male Roman Catholic descendant living. The English branch of the family is really the elder; as [Charles Boileau](Charles%20Boileau%20%28d.1733%29.md), eldest son of Jacques, who died after ten years in prison for his faith, resigned the Castelnau property, being a Protestant, at the [Revocation of the Edict of Nantes](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Nantes#Revocation), in 1598* [Emily makes an error here: the edict was *passed* in 1598, but *revoked* in 1685]*, in favour of his younger brother 'Maurice', a Roman Catholic and migrated to England. Here he entered the Army, and his sons and grandsons, [Simeon](Simeon%20Boileau%20%281717-1767%29.md), [Solomon](Solomon%20Boileau%20%281745-1810%29.md), and others settled in Ireland where your Great Grandmother, née [Alicia Boileau](Alicia%20Boileau%20%281779-1851%29.md) was born in 1780, being the 17th child! My French relations were very cordial; and said how glad they would be at any time to welcome members of the English branch; and that [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) was the first they had seen of the younger generation. At lunch with the de Valfons we met their only son Henri the future Marquis - only married a fortnight - who expressed a hope that Jessie would go and see them on her honeymoon!*

*Thence we moved on to pretty [Hyéres](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hy%C3%A8res), picturesque with palms; and to [Valescure](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?f=q&source=s_q&hl=en&geocode=&q=Valescure,+Fr%C3%A9jus,+France&sll=43.436218,6.765347&sspn=0.063447,0.154324&ie=UTF8&hq=&hnear=Valescure,+Fr%C3%A9jus,+Var,+Provence-Alpes-C%C3%B4te+d'Azur,+France&t=h&z=14), on the Estelle Mts. whose slopes were clothed with [arbutus](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbutus) trees 20 feet high, gay with their crimson and golden bell fruit. We spent Christmas at '[Mauvarre](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?q=Mauvarre,+near+Cannes&hl=en&ie=UTF8&hq=&hnear=Boulevard+de+Mauvarre,+06400+Cannes,+Alpes-Maritimes,+Provence-Alpes-C%C3%B4te+d'Azur,+France&ll=43.549792,7.052536&spn=0.063328,0.154324&z=13)', Cannes; and the New Year 1900 dawned upon us at [Mentone](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menton), a sweet reposeful place* [footnote by [JEBB](Lt.%20Col.%20Ted%20Barton%20%281897-1971%29.md): *Not quite so reposeful in 1963!*]*, which we greatly prefer to [Cannes](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannes), [Nice](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nice), [Monte Carlo](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monte_Carlo) or any others of the gay resorts of fashion on the Riviera.*

*One afternoon we went up by a funicular railway the mountain side to [La Turbia](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Turbie), an old Roman tower commanding the coast above [Monaco](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monaco); and walked thence along the old Cornice 'Vetturino' (coach) road to see the site of my dear Mother's sketch of [Esa](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%88ze), a Saracenic tower, pitched on a natural sugar-loaf shaped rock, rising 500 or 600 feet, from the level of the sea out of the valley. I had seen Esa last in 1862 when I sat beside your [Grandmother](Emily%20Dougan%20%281806-1877%29.md) on the coupé of a 'vetturino' from which she drew it. Those were the days before the railway came, when that beautiful coast was known only to travellers* **{p116}<a name="116"></a>** *by carriage, or 'diligence', as one sometimes wishes it still might be. On our way to [Genoa](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genoa) and [Pisa](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pisa) we passed [Bordighera](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bordighera), [San Remo](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanremo), [Spezzia](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Spezia), [Alassio](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alassio) and other well known places. At Pisa we spent a night to see the famous [Leaning Tower](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leaning_Tower_of_Pisa), [Baptistry](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptistry_(Pisa)), [Duomo](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duomo_di_Pisa#Duomo), and [Campo Santo](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Santo) surrounding the last; made of earth brought in ship-loads from the Holy Land, wherein to lay the precious dust of the sacred Dead.*

*Our goal now was [Rome](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rome) - where on January 20 our [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) was to join us; and who courageously undertook the long journey from England out to us alone, as she did not meet with an escort to suit her date. She appeared, as she always does, safe and sound, bright and bonny; and threw herself into sightseeing of antiquities, Churches and galleries with her usual zest.*

*It was exactly 40 years since I spent seven months in the 'Eternal City' as a girl with my [parents](Rev.%20Charles%20Boileau%20Elliott%20%281803-1875%29.md) and Aunt [Alicia](Alicia%20Elliott%20%281832-1912%29.md); and I was interested to see how much I remembered of Rome's many historic interests, despite all I had seen in the interim in the Far East. The [Coliseum](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colosseum), and [Forum](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Forum), [Arch of Titus](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arch_of_Titus), and the [Capitol](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitoline_Hill); the [Vatican Gallery](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vatican_Museums) of pictures, and the great [Cathedral of St. Peters](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Peter's_Basilica), besides many other churches, and the Early Christians' [Catacombs](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catacombs_of_Rome); are memories that can never die. Of all these [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) has written most graphically in her journal of letters to the Family. We spent five delightful weeks in Rome, at the [Hotel Beau Site](http://www.beausiterome.com/index.php), on the [Pincian Hill](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pincian_Hill).*

*The [Boer War](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Anglo-Boer_War) was now in full swing, and we heard of the fatal 'black week' of disasters from the columns of the friendly Italian papers - and every evening after table d'hote the English gentlemen used to wait outside on the steps as the newsboys came swinging round the corner, sending their musical voices before them, calling out 'Tribuna'! and buying their papers.*

*After Rome we spent a week at [Naples](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naples) and [Castellamare](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castellammare_di_Stabia) seeing [Sorrento](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorrento), [Amalfi](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amalfi) and [Pompei](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pompeii); and thence our Quartett went to [Florence](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence), where dear Marcie Rickard met us; and later [Isa](Isabel%20Mary%20Young%20%28b.1870%29.md) and [Vere Monro](Archibald%20Vere%20Monro.md). In some ways one can enjoy Florence more than Rome; because the former is more compact, and distances not so great. Our comfortable and reasonable Pension 'Ricciolli' on the banks of the [River Arno](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arno), gave us a good view of [St. Miniato](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Miniato_al_Monte) across the River. The hill is crowned by this beautiful Church, and by Michael Angelo's fine bronze* *statue of [David](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_(Michelangelo))* [[Donatello's David](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_(Donatello)) was bronze, but Michelangelo's was stone]*, when young. We were fond of going to [Fiesole](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiesole) by tram; and made friends with Mr. Venables, the very able though deaf preacher at the American Anglican service which we attended.* **{p117}<a name="117"></a>**

[[Embedded YouTube video]](https://www.youtube.com/embed/2WmfKnLXg2Y?fs=1)

*Florence is a City of flowers and of Bells - I could not venture to say how many Church 'Campaniles' ring out the quarter hours; and when the '[Angelus](http://www.newadvent.org/cathen/01487a.htm)' sounds at mid-day and at 6 p.m. Italians always cross themselves, bow the head, and repeat the '[Gloria Patri](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloria_Patri)'; as in the well known [picture](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Francois_Millet#The_Angelus)* [below] *which has immortalized this custom, wherein the young man and maiden are represented as arrested in their work in the fields, by the sound of the mid-day 'Angelus' bells from the distant village church tower**.* *The wealth of colours in the flowers mounted on gallery-stands at every street corner baffles description. [Mimosa](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimosa) of delicate scent and primrose tint - [Anemones](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anemone) - scarlet purple and white - [Iris](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iris_(plant)) golden and mauve, and [Roses](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose) in profusion abounded in March. One very marked difference between Florence and Rome struck us pleasantly, which I must mention before I go on. That is the total absence of priests and nuns to be met in the streets of the former city; whereas in Rome they swarm. Seminaries and schools seem to abound in the 'Eternal City'; where morning and afternoon we met 'crocodiles' of priestlings, from 12 to 20 years old dressed in either scarlet or black cossacks, who were out for their constitutional walks. In Florence no such sight is to be seen. The explanation given is that [King Victor Emmanuel](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Emmanuel_II_of_Italy) (grandfather of the [present King](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Emmanuel_III_of_Italy)) insisted on all Monasteries being closed in Florence; as he found that they were schools of sedition, and bred more anarchists than theologians.*

[![external image Jean-Fran%C3%A7ois_Millet_%28II%29_001.jpg](http://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d7/Jean-Fran%C3%A7ois_Millet_%28II%29_001.jpg "external image Jean-Fran%C3%A7ois_Millet_%28II%29_001.jpg")](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Francois_Millet#The_Angelus)\
'The Angelus', by Jean-François Millet, now at the [Musée d'Orsay](http://www.musee-orsay.fr/index.php?id=851&L=1&tx_commentaire_pi1%5BshowUid%5D=339&no_cache=1).

*Easter fell on April 15 this year* [1900] *- and the following day we moved on to [Venice](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venice); which was unknown to all of us but the Father. Our expectations were great; but none of us were disappointed by that romantic silent city of water streets; and [St. Mark's Cathedral](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_St._Mark) which gives the whole story of the Bible in marvellous mosaics on its walls exceeded our anticipation.*

*One day we took a gondola and rowed to an [Island](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murano) to see the famous [Salviati Glass Factory](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Salviati). And another day went to the Lace Factory, and saw all the women at work. No machine lace to be seen there! but priceless old patterns being copied or mended. Bent iron work is another of the many beautiful Venetian industries; and gold and silver filigree, and mosaics also. Venice struck me as a far more artistic place altogether than either Rome or Florence; and but for the unsavoury odours which pervade the canals, nothing could surpass the charm of our memories of Venice.* **{p118}<a name="118"></a>**

*On [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md)'s birthday April 21* [1900] *we left this lovely place with its matchless interests, historical, architectural and artistic, for the [Lake of Como](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Como); and spent a beautiful month on its banks, at peaceful [Cadenabbia](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadenabbia). Three days later [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) returned to England to keep [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) company again and to carry on her editorial duties for 'On Service' and for the 'Time and Talents News'; and Father saw her off from [Lugano](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lugano). She had a lonely and somewhat eventful journey, but her usual pluck carried her through!*

*On May 3rd* [1900] *[Father](Rev.%20John%20Barton%20of%20Cambridge%20%281836-1908%29.md), [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) and I embarked on one of the Lake steamers, and got off at a typical little Italian village called [Nesso](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nesso), perched on a hillside, with a long flight of flagged stone steps leading ever higher and higher. We were on the look out for a steamer coming down the Lake from Como, which was to bring our [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) and his [wife](Lilian%20Wyatt%20%28b.1870%29.md) from India. We kept sitting down on the low wall to rest; and then mounted a little higher, and talked to the peasant women basket-making in the sun, ever and anon looking back for the Boat we meant to board, and return with our dear ones to* [Cadenabbia](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadenabbia)*. Suddenly in the bright afternoon sun the little steamer rounded a sharp corner a mile off, and we quickly retraced our steps again to the rustic little village pier. As the Boat drew alongside, there was our Arthur, as large as life, standing on the Bridge waving to us! We three were soon on board, and a very happy and lovely fortnight followed at Cadenabbia with Arthur and Lilian as guests. Most afternoons we crossed the Lake in a rowing boat, to different Latières for our tea; and scrambled up the hillside after gentians and other wild flowers, before returning at sundown in time for table d'hote.*

*[Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) was lost in admiration of the beauty of the [azaleas](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azalea) at the [Villa Carlotta](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villa_Carlotta), which are at their best in May; the sloping lawns thickly studded with bushes whose colouring was more like brilliant sunset clouds dropped to earth than anything terrestrial; shaded from scarlet to palest pink; and from orange to softest primrose. We could hardly get him away! The pergola outside our hotel, and the walls for a mile along the Lakeside were festooned with luxuriant wisteria creeper, and cream-coloured banksia rose, which combination of colour charmed my eyes quite as much as the Villa Carlotta gardens.* **{p119}<a name="119"></a>**

*The lovely fortnight was soon over, and our 'Indians' had to go on to England; and [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) and we left* *[Cadenabbia](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadenabbia)* *for [Riva](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riva_del_Garda), on [Lake Garda](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Garda), an as yet unknown Paradise to us all. This little town has risen up at the further, and narrow end of the Lake partly owing to hot salubrious springs having been found to attract Italians in search of health during July and August. Some splendid red peaks of Dolomite rock stand as sentinels on either side of the quiet township; rising perpendicular out of the Lake about 1000 feet. So steep indeed that our little steamer went gliding along half a stone's throw from them in sapphire coloured waters that deepened to indigo as we drew up to the pier. The Beau Rivage Hotel where we had bespoken rooms, is nearly a mile out of Riva; and so early as the end of May, is not patronized by many English. We only met a Mrs. and Miss Eliot there, who have since taken up work in South London. (Mrs. Eliot is sister to [Dr. Davidson](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randall_Davidson) now Archbishop of Canterbury). They were glad to join us in a little service Father arranged for [Ascension Day](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascension_Day#Feast).*

*The weather was balmy, not yet too hot; and everyone breakfasted outside, at little round tables; and we feasted our eyes on the abundant flowers around, and on the glorious Dolomites overhead. We generally chose a table that faced down a pergola 100 yards or more long, gay with roses, and honeysuckle; bounded on either side by a tall hedge of purple [iris](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iris_(plant)), which led up to a plantation of grey olive trees; and the vista closed with a peep of the cobalt blue waters of [Lake Garda](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Garda) in the distance, After breakfast we took out books, and writing materials, and sat for hours under the olives, watching the sailing boats that passed. The unstudied artistic effect of a large flopping terracotta sail gliding past; or of a smaller boat painted emerald green with a primrose coloured sail to carry it along upon those blue, blue waters, under cloudless skies, spoke loud of the inborn taste of these, simple Italian fisher-folk. Returning to the hotel in time for déjeuner, we wandered on grassy footpaths beside a streamlet that flowed between four feet deep walls, covered with moss and fern, and a particularly fine forget-me-not, that revelled in this cool damp. Once or twice our footsteps, or voices, startled an exquisite creature whose black eyes gleamed up at us from out the luxuriantly covered walls. It was a large lizard, or newt, of a bright emerald colour, with an azure blue throat!* **{p120}<a name="120"></a>**

*After ten restful days at Riva we travelled by a little hill railway line to [Toblach](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toblach), 50 miles off; and there met [Lilford Causton](Lilford%20Jervoise%20Causton%20%28b.1873%29.md), May Steward* [footnote by [JEBB](Lt.%20Col.%20Ted%20Barton%20%281897-1971%29.md): *Sister of Margaret, for some years governess to the 'Cecil' family*]*, and Eva Paddon, from England. They made a very happy addition to our party, and it was a joy to see their delight over the blue [gentians](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentiana) that studded the grass; and over the [edelweiss](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edelweiss) that grew as thick as poppies amongst the hay! We drove the same day on to [Cortina](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cortina_d'Ampezzo), 20 miles off, and found it a veritable garden of flowers during June. We used to say that we spent this month beside seas of pink '[primula farinosa](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primula_farinosa)'; lakes of golden '[trollias](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trollius)'; and streams of '[forget-me-nots](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forget-me-not)'. Never before or since have I seen such a wealth of rare wild flowers; and the many I painted during these summer months shews how we all loved them, for dear Father and the young ones, who walked much, often brought me in three or four a day to paint. It was a sad break in our party when Lilford was sent to return to England on account of the death of his Vicar's wife, a cousin of mine, née [Ethel Reid](Ethel%20Emma%20Louise%20Reid%20%281860-1900%29.md). Cortina is only three miles from the frontier into Austria, who is bringing pressure to bear on the people to induce them to adopt both the German language and current coinage - guldens and pfennig. All boys and men are obliged by law to learn German; whereas the peasant women hold fast by their belovéd native language, Italian. Therefore when shopping, you must be prepared to speak in either tongue; and reckon for your purchases in either of the two current coinages - lire or gulden. As evening closed in and we returned from our walks, I often noticed that the men saluted us with 'guten abend'; but the women with 'buona sera', or 'felice notte'.*

*At the end of June* [1900] *we made our way to the [Riffel Alp](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?f=q&source=s_q&hl=en&geocode=&q=Station+Riffelalp,+Zermatt,+Suisse&sll=46.004221,7.753129&sspn=0.007214,0.01929&gl=uk&ie=UTF8&hq=&hnear=Riffelalp,+3920+Zermatt,+Visp,+Valais,+Switzerland&ll=46.004668,7.754116&spn=0.007586,0.01929&z=16), via beautiful [Insbruck](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innsbruck), [Zurich](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurich), and across the [Fourka Pass](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Furka_Pass) and the [Rhone Glacier](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rh%C3%B4ne_Glacier). We saw May Steward off to England from Zurich, and Eva Paddon remained another fortnight with us. [Father](Rev.%20John%20Barton%20of%20Cambridge%20%281836-1908%29.md) took the English Chaplaincy during July at the Riffel Alp, as he had done during June at Cortina; and we became very fond of the little English Church, where Daily Morning Prayer was held at 8 a.m., [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) acting organist. Over the West door is illuminated 'O Ice and Snow bless ye the Lord!' and through a small oriel window above this door one sees the majestic [Matterhorn](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matterhorn) surrounded by the eternal snows of lesser peaks.* **{p121]<a name="121"></a>**

*I painted a good many more flowers here; amongst others the rare large blue columbine ([aquilegia](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquilegia)) whose little known abode Father discovered - also a tiny hairy [forget-me-not](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forget-me-not), an inch high, on the summit of the [Gorner Grat](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gornergrat), which we ascended by the funicular railway. After spending a month here, during which time Father and Jessie went once or twice on the glaciers, we spent August* [1900] *at [Montana](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana,_Switzerland) and Finshant* [?]*, meeting dear [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) again, and [Edie Cane](Edith%20Frances%20Cane%20%28b.1869%29.md), at [Sierre](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierre). In September we were at [Chamonix](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamonix), and at [Clarens](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarens,_Switzerland) for a fortnight, and then returned, with thankful hearts to Wimbledon for a few days, to leave boxes, and repack.*

# Abinger, 1900-1901

*As our tenants there wished to spend another winter at Castelnau, Father undertook to take duty at [Abinger](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abinger), near Dorking, for six months. Before settling there however, we went to [Strathfieldsaye](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratfield_Saye) in Berkshire - to the home of [Isa Monro](Isabel%20Mary%20Young%20%28b.1870%29.md)'s father-in-law, for a month; a delightful arrangement, as we thus saw a good deal of dear Isa, and her little [Margaret](Margaret%20Susan%20Monro%20%28b.1898%29.md) - though [Vere](Archibald%20Vere%20Monro.md) was away. It was very interesting to see the big [house and park](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratfield_Saye_House) given by the nation to the [Duke of Wellington](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington) in recognition of his services to his country at [Waterloo](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Waterloo) in 1815. The house was empty, the great Duke's successor and nephew, having recently died - but the old butler told us that the place was very rheumatic, as it lies low. This old gentleman shewed us some rare heirlooms - telling of past glory, and exquisite Spanish china, brought by the old Duke from the Peninsular War etc. One room was panelled with large paintings, each representing the members of different Peers' families who were at the Coronation of [King George IV](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_IV_of_the_United_Kingdom) in 1820. Of course the Wellesley family were among the number thus immortalised - and the colouring of the peeresses velvet dresses and trains, and the jewels in their and the peers' coronets were marvellously done.*

*Our winter at* [Abinger](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abinger) *Rectory would have been a great success; had not the beloved Father made the mistake of undertaking the duties of the Central Secretary of C.M.S. (temporarily) as well. This involved being away from Abinger, and travelling over the country, constantly from Tuesday to Friday every week; or else going to London and back daily. The wear and tear proved too great - and he was obliged to resign this C.M.S. work in May 1901 - but he never really got over the strain of attempting to combine the two works.* **{p122}<a name="122"></a>**

*Nevertheless we all look back with pleasure on this winter at* [Abinger](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abinger) *Rectory - a most picturesque house, and ideal garden. Uncle [Handley](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) and Aunt [Mary Moule](Mary%20Elliott%20%28b.1844%29.md), [Tessie](Tesie%20Moule%20%281882-1905%29.md) and [Isa](Isabel%20Catherine%20Moule%20%281884-1959%29.md) all spent Christmas with us. Dear Tessie arrived with a nasty cough and bad throat which proved to be the beginning of lung trouble; and two months after began life in a Sanatorium at [Bexhill](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bexhill-on-Sea); and after four and a half years of trial 'in the School of Suffering' entered into the inheritance of the Saints of Light as is most beautifully told in the memoir written by her parents under the above title.*

*Our little grandson [Jackie](Jackie%20Barton%20%281896-1904%29.md) too, will ever be remembered as part of our* [Abinger](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abinger) *life - for he was with us for eleven weeks at the Rectory. Our [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) was at this time Chaplain at the Port of London, for [Seamen's Mission](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mission_to_Seafarers); and living at [Poplar](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poplar,_London); and as his little son had developed glandular trouble in his neck, the doctor forbade East End London climate for him. He had two operations that spring; which involved frequent dressings and bandagings. But no child could have given less trouble - or more pleasure to us all. It was pathetic to hear him say 'I'm such a little boy to have so many troubles!' He stayed with us until his fifth birthday, May 22* [1901]*, and we little thought then, that more than half his sweet life had already run. I have never known a child with so sensitive a conscience; or such a combination of manliness with love of beauty, colour, flowers, and scenery.*

*His [Father](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) cycled from* [Poplar](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poplar,_London) *to* [Abinger](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abinger) *and back in the day four times whilst little [Jackie](Jackie%20Barton%20%281896-1904%29.md) was with us; and although the dear child was absolutely happy in the country with us, yet of course he longed on such days to go home with his Father, and to take, instead of send, the best golden buttercups he had picked for his Mother. But - never a tear - as he watched his Father's receding cycle, and squeezed one of our hands very hard. He said many quaint, as well as pretty things. Once 'How I do wish we could Poplar into Abinger! that would be lovely - but Poplar is so dirty - when I go back I must take a big piece of soap and wash Poplar!' (Rather a big order!) He walked all round a little plot of moss one day rather than put his foot on it - 'It is too beautiful - I cannot step on it'. When bluebells carpeted the woods, we picked and sent a good many* **{p123}<a name="123"></a>** *to Bermondsey factory girls and others. Jackie exclaimed once 'Oh don't step on the bluebells, they are just like heaven!' 'Do you know why lilies of the valley are my favourites? because they always seem to speak of God.' And now both these dear young saints are safely gathered in 'Where ever lasting spring abides and never withering flowers'.*

![Captioned Abinger May 7 1901.jpg](../files/Captioned%20Abinger%20May%207%201901.jpg "Captioned Abinger May 7 1901.jpg")\
At Abinger Rectory, 7 May 1901.

# Return to Wimbledon, 1901

*The [Arthurs](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) had spent this winter at 'Belcamp' Wimbledon en famille, and in May* [1901] *we returned to Castelnau, and were glad to be once more in our own home again after 18 months absence. We had Arthur and his family with us for a while; and at the end of July he and [Lilian](Lilian%20Wyatt%20%28b.1870%29.md) accompanied Father and me to Ewenny Priory. Coloneland Mrs.Turbervill got up a garden meeting for Father to speak of the many needs of great Mining Districts around, and where Welsh clergy are liberally assisted by the C.P.A. grants. Their beautiful lawns and spreading trees lent themselves well to the gathering. We had lovely weather at Ewenny, and archery and tennis went on daily; and a long table spread under a noble oak with tea for thirsty players, and dishes of magnificent peaches, and grapes was typical of English countryside hospitality. Our friend Beatrice Picton-Warlow was at home; her twin sister was at [Simla](Shimla%2C%20India.md) working for the Y.W.C.A.*

*On August 1st* [1901] *[Lilian](Lilian%20Wyatt%20%28b.1870%29.md) went to Suffolk to her parents and children, and [Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) accompanied us to [Ambleside](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambleside), where (as often before) the family gathered for that month. We took Kelsick Villa, which accommodated six or seven of us, and [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) and [Will](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) had 'diggings' out. The photo group taken with Arthur's large camera gives seven out of our octave - [Cecil](Rev.%20Cecil%20Barton%20%281870-1909%29.md) being in India, was the only absentee; and Will's dear dog 'Dimmi' is included. I cannot recall the many excursions of that summer, but you will all remember the dear 'Dad's' last climb up and down [Scafell](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sca_Fell), in which you stalwart young ones had greatly to assist him - and how one of your climbing acquaintances called him a real 'Patriarch' in appearance. Nevertheless - it was a fine close to his many scores of climbs in the Lake District during 44 years, to ascend that mountain with five sons and a daughter at the age of 64.*

![Ambleside August 1901 Arthur Jack Fred Ethel Jess Guy Bill (Cecil was in India).jpg](../files/Ambleside%20August%201901%20Arthur%20Jack%20Fred%20Ethel%20Jess%20Guy%20Bill%20%28Cecil%20was%20in%20India%29.jpg "Ambleside August 1901 Arthur Jack Fred Ethel Jess Guy Bill (Cecil was in India).jpg")\
[Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) (aged 37), [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) (35), [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) (33), [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md) (30), [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) (27), [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) (26) and [Bill](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) (24) at Ambleside, 1901

*Another fine day, after [Fred](Fred%20Barton%20%281868-1929%29.md) had left us, we went to [Ullswater](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ullswater) - Father and I on a coach - escorted by six on cycles. After picnicing on one of the shady promontories on the Lake, we rowed to a tiny rocky island, and put the quintett of [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md), [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md), [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) and [Will](Bill%20Barton%20%281877-1952%29.md) to sing to us.* **{p124}<a name="124"></a>** *[Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) rowed us parents a short distance out on the Lake, as we listened to the Concert they gave us from the top of the Cliff - of Mendelssohn's Open Air Part Songs, ending with 'Like a river glorious', and 'Lead, kindly Light'. Other boats stopped also to listen - to the melodious voices of brothers and sister as they reached us across the water. As I look back and recall the love and union that reigned amongst us all during these summer gatherings, I thank God for the strong cement in family life which is built on a foundation that will stand all weathers.*

*It was during this month at* [Ambleside](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambleside) *that we heard that dear Uncle [Handley](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) had been offered and accepted the Bishopric of Durham; and although this would involve leaving Cambridge where he had spent 40 most happy and useful years, we could not but rejoice at this acknowledgement, by the highest in the land, of our dear Brother's great intellectual and spiritual gifts. After a visit to the Sholto Douglases, at Douglas Support, and to the Learmonths at Park Hall, we reached York in time for Uncle's consecration at the [Minster](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/York_Minster) on October 18* [1901]*, St.Luke's Day; a wonderfully beautiful and solemn service.*

*This month* [October 1901] *[Arthur](Arthur%20Elliott%20Barton%20%281864-1931%29.md) and [Lilian](Lilian%20Wyatt%20%28b.1870%29.md) returned to India with their small boy [Leslie](Leslie%20Barton%20%281900-1928%29.md), aged one year. They were stationed first at [Ambala](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambala), where Arthur suffered terribly from [sciatica](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sciatica) for five months; and a year later went to [Ferozepore](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firozpur) as 'pukka' Deputy Commissioner - with a District larger than Yorkshire to govern.*

*The winter that followed was our last at Castelnau, Wimbledon. The dear [Moules](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) were very unsettled, as [Tessie](Tesie%20Moule%20%281882-1905%29.md) was ordered abroad with a nurse, and Aunt [Mary](Mary%20Elliott%20%28b.1844%29.md) followed her there in January after an accés of the dear girl's illness. [Auckland Castle](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auckland_Castle) was found to need such extensive repairs, that it was not habitable for a year; and Uncle [Handley](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) and [Lilford Causton](Lilford%20Jervoise%20Causton%20%28b.1873%29.md) (then Domestic Chaplain) lived at the Lodge; whence Lilford added to his clerical duties those of 'clerk of the works' in order to see that the new drainage worked, and water and gas supplies were all thoroughly well done; as well as a Library arranged, and kitchens changed.*

*In March 1902 Aunt [Mary](Mary%20Elliott%20%28b.1844%29.md) had to undergo a serious operation at [Cannes](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannes); and as the Uncle could not possibly leave England that month, dear Father offered to go, and send him daily reports, wires and letters. This was the* **{p125}<a name="125"></a>** *last of many acts of the kind that Father did in the family - and the last long journey he took alone.*

*On March 25* [1902] *your Uncle [Willy Young](Sir%20William%20Mackworth%20Young%20%28b.1840%29.md) and Aunt [May](Frances%20May%20Egerton.md) arrived from India, and I had the great pleasure of receiving them at Castelnau late that Sunday evening. I was not surprised that the dear Uncle was very tired, after the 30 hours' journey from Marseilles! for as Aunt May put it, he naturally felt the fatigue a good deal of having to look after the luggage etc. himself when he had been 'waited upon hand and foot for 39 years in the East! ' But you all know how wonderfully they have accommodated themselves since then to English life and conditions in their home at [Oak Lea, St. Leonard's](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?f=q&source=s_q&hl=en&geocode=&q=oak+lee,+St+Leonards&sll=50.822638,-1.845188&sspn=0.027599,0.077162&ie=UTF8&hq=&hnear=Oak+Ln,+United+Kingdom&ll=51.749989,-0.65918&spn=0.027047,0.077162&z=14), and how Aunt May with her pen, and Uncle Willy by being President of the [Church of England Zenana Society](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_England_Zenana_Mission). and by advocating the cause of Missions, are continuing to use the great influence they acquired in India.*

*[Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) and I spent Easter* [1902] *at [Poplar](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poplar,_London), with [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) and [Susie](Susan%20Thornton%20%281875-1958%29.md), and much enjoyed his Lantern Service for Sailors on Good Friday - with beautiful slides - and illustrated songs sung by himself. Then we went to [Auckland](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auckland_Castle), where the dear Father joined us from [Cannes](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannes); and we spent six or seven very happy weeks with Uncle [Handley](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) at the Lodge there. [Lilford Causton](Lilford%20Jervoise%20Causton%20%28b.1873%29.md) (who was at that time his domestic chaplain) and Jessie sorted papers left by the [Westcott](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooke_Westcott) family, and books ad infinitum. It was now brought home to me that the dear Father's power for work was failing - and it was startling to hear him say as he put aside a packet of papers we had asked him to look through 'I can't do it.'*

*The first week in May* [1902] *Uncle [Handley](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) and your [Father](Rev.%20John%20Barton%20of%20Cambridge%20%281836-1908%29.md) went South for special meetings in London; and Uncle [Willy Young](Sir%20William%20Mackworth%20Young%20%28b.1840%29.md) met his two brothers-in-law on the C.M.S. Platform in [Exeter Hall](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exeter_Hall), the dear Uncle from India making his maiden speech on Foreign Missions, and the Bishop-Uncle giving the concluding address. It was the first, and last time, the three stood together on the Platform. Just at this time Aunt [Mary](Mary%20Elliott%20%28b.1844%29.md) returned from* [Cannes](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannes) *with [Tessie](Tesie%20Moule%20%281882-1905%29.md) - and Father went with the former to see the Sanatorium at [Mundesley](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mundesley), in Norfolk, where the dear girl now went for ten months. We all spent Trinity Sunday - May 25* [1902] *- at [Durham](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durham), with Canon and Mrs. Tristram at the Close. This was Uncle Handley's first ordination in his own Cathedral* **{p126}<a name="126"></a>** *-* *a glorious and solemn service, and [Claude Thornton](Rev.%20Claude%20Cecil%20Thornton%20%281844-1921%29.md) was gospeller. In the evening Aunt Mary shewed us the Uncle's suite of rooms in Durham Castle, where historic interests and old oak abound; and we were present at a service for the newly ordained in the Castle Chapel.*

*The last days of May* [1902] *we went into Teesdale, taking [Lilford](Lilford%20Jervoise%20Causton%20%28b.1873%29.md) with us, for four days, and Father was delighted to find four different Alpine flowers growing wild here and set me to paint them as in olden days; the [gentian verna](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentiana_verna), golden [trollias](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trollius), [primula farinosa](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primula_farinosa), and wild purple large [pansy](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pansy). One day he and Lilford went a ten mile trudge over the moors, and he came back rather 'done' - but this was not surprising. On June 2nd we drove to see the [Forse](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Force) and Falls at [Middleton](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middleton-in-Teesdale), on which well known lines have been written - and [Turner's painting](http://nga.gov.au/exhibition/turnertomonet/Detail.cfm?IRN=3528&ViewID=2) of which is in the National Gallery. We had arranged to meet Uncle Handley here; and as we caught sight of him, he waved a white handkerchief, cheering; and told us the news that rejoiced the whole world the previous evening - namely that [Peace had been proclaimed in South Africa](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Vereeniging) on June 1st.*

[![external image 3528.jpg](http://nga.gov.au/exhibition/turnertomonet/Images/LRG/3528.jpg "external image 3528.jpg")](http://nga.gov.au/exhibition/turnertomonet/Detail.cfm?IRN=3528&ViewID=2)\
'High Force, fall of the Tees, Yorkshire' by [J. M. W. Turner](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._M._W._Turner).

*Soon after this we returned to Wimbledon, and spent a quiet summer, preparatory to giving up 'Castelnau' at Michelmas. During July* [1902] *we migrated to [Tattingstone Rectory](Tattingstone%20Rectory%2C%20Suffolk.md) for two or three weeks, where [Jack](Rev.%20Jack%20Barton%20%281866-1941%29.md) and his family joined us, and he and Father shared the duty. Jack took me in a trailer about the village, and I saw about 30 old friends amongst the inhabitants thus, which was a great pleasure. I have not seen that dear old Home since those sweet summer days.*

*On September 25 [1902] we left 'Castelnau' which had been a very pleasant home for nearly nine years, though not one for which we had any real affection. Father had a severe headache I remember; and left his bed with difficulty to go up Wimbledon Hill in a cab to Fred's home* [[27 Lingfield Road, Wimbledon](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?f=q&source=s_q&hl=en&geocode=&q=27+Lingfield+Road,+wimbledon&sll=51.424046,-0.21904&sspn=0.003405,0.009645&gl=uk&ie=UTF8&hq=&hnear=27+Lingfield+Rd,+Merton,+Greater+London+SW19+4,+United+Kingdom&z=16)]*; and that night we had another warning that our [Dear One](Rev.%20John%20Barton%20of%20Cambridge%20%281836-1908%29.md)'s health was failing! for he had some sort of internal haemorrhage. In October, having warehoused our furniture for six months, we went to [Mark Ash [House], Abinger](http://www.cqout.com/item.asp?id=4920712), for 10 weeks; and thence Father continued to seek for a home that would suit us for the remainder of our days.* **{p127}<a name="127"></a>**

# Weybridge, 1903

*We saw houses at [Guildford](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guildford), [Tunbridge Wells](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunbridge_Wells), [Kenley](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenley), [Broxbourne](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broxbourne), [Woking](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woking) and [Weybridge](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weybridge); and finally decided to take a furnished one in the last named place, from February 1st 1903 for two months, so as to be on the spot if a house here fell vacant that would suit us all. And thus our 'lines fell in this pleasant place' at [Moorcroft](https://maps.google.co.uk/maps?f=q&source=s_q&hl=en&geocode=&q=moorcroft,+elgin+road,+weybridge,+surrey&sll=51.364995,-0.457778&sspn=0.003242,0.009645&gl=uk&ie=UTF8&hq=&hnear=Moorcroft,+Elgin+Rd,+Weybridge,+Surrey+KT13+8TR,+United+Kingdom&ll=51.364386,-0.459216&spn=0.006819,0.01929&z=16), which has been our home now for four years. (I write **1907**).*

***January 1908**. We had spent Christmas 1902 at [Thornhaugh, Croydon](Thornhaugh%2C%20Croydon.md), the dear Aunts' home; and it was there I first saw the small cloud arise in our sky into which it was God's will that we should now enter. Dear [Father](Rev.%20John%20Barton%20of%20Cambridge%20%281836-1908%29.md) tried to write a birthday letter to Uncle [Handley](Rev.%20Handley%20Moule%20%281841-1920%29.md) on December 22nd, and found that he could not control his pen, although he tried on three different sheets of paper. He smiled over it - but my heart told me this was serious; and as I look back now five years, I would give thanks for the loving kindness that has been dealt out to us all; in that the beloved Father's failure has not only been so gradual, but so painless.*

*We saved him all possible exertion and trouble on getting into 'Moorcroft'; and [Jessie](Jessie%20Barton%20%281874-1955%29.md) and he went to the old home '[East Leigh](East%20Leigh%20House%2C%20Havant.md)' for a few days whilst [Ethel](Ethel%20Barton%20%281871-1937%29.md), [Guy](Guy%20Barton%20%281875-1947%29.md) and I managed the move together; and Father and Jess returned home on April 9* [1903] *so that we might spend Easter together here on the 12th in the new home, which we realized would also be our last home on earth.
